# Sci\-Fin — Product & Technical Specification

**Status:** living document, consolidated from the research audit, two HTML prototype iterations, and an extended design conversation. Sections are marked **Decided**, **Open**, or **Deferred** — treat anything not marked Decided as unsettled, even if it reads confidently. **As of the 2026\-09\-02 pre\-implementation audit, every section is Decided or explicitly Deferred, and ~~the five~~ the genuinely open questions are listed together in §13.1**, which alone keeps their count (corrected 2026\-09\-25) — that audit was the pass §14 had been calling for, checking each Decided section against the build\-from\-spec bar below rather than against its own internal consistency. §13 is now split so the live list, the deferred work, the implementation backlog and the dated decision trail are four separate things.

**Reference artifacts:**

- `sci-fin-research-audit.md` — the science\-backed audit of the original app (BudgetFlow) this project replaces; §2 of this spec summarizes its findings, but the audit itself is the primary source for the research citations
- `sci-fin-prototype.html` — the current interactive HTML prototype ~~(ground truth for pixel\-level detail: exact copy, exact layout, exact interaction flow)~~ (the interaction reference and the second oracle for the seeded engine figures, **frozen at 2026\-09\-14 — anything decided after that date overrides it** (§14); corrected 2026\-09\-25, when the audit found it still carrying the removed text\-size row and predating the trend window and the Reports labels, and its Age of Money figure short of §5.1's deficit rule, §13.3). Supersedes the earlier `sci-fin-stage2-prototype.html`, which is kept only as a historical snapshot *(not in the project folder as of 2026\-09\-25)*.
- **Code\-writing phase note (added 2026\-07\-28):** once implementation begins, engineering must build strictly from this spec document — not from `sci-fin-prototype.html`. The prototype's role is limited to what the bullet above describes: validating visual layout and interaction flow. It is deliberately seeded with fictional demo data (goal names, dollar figures, transaction history, account balances, dates) purely so it's navigable as a mockup, and it inevitably carries mockup\-only implementation choices (shortcuts, quick fixes, occasional bugs) that were never meant to define the product. None of that is a specification of anything. It must never be copied into the production codebase as a default, seed content, or placeholder data — and where the prototype's specifics aren't explicitly Decided in this document, this document governs, not the prototype's current behavior. This spec is the one artifact meant to stay pristine and load\-bearing for implementation; treat any gap as a reason to resolve it here first, not to fall back on whatever the prototype happens to do.

* * *

## 1\. Overview

Sci\-Fin is a personal budgeting app built specifically to correct the failure modes the research audit found in typical budgeting apps: punitive red/negative styling, grading, streak pressure, and an assumption that everyone's financial life starts from a paycheck. Its foundational primitive is **Savings**, not income — the app is meant to work as well for someone building from an existing pool of money as for someone with a regular monthly paycheck, and to remain useful and non\-punitive for someone in real financial distress, not just someone routinely on\-budget.

**Platform scope: Android and Windows 10/11 (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — previously stated as Android\-only, which the rest of the document had already outgrown).** Two target platforms, no iOS. This matters in three places the document already depends on: §3.9's New Month notification is a genuine platform commitment on *both* targets (scheduling, permission handling, and recovery across a reboot — Android's alarm/work scheduling plus a Windows equivalent, not one implementation), §7.3's rejection of SMS/notification auto\-detection now rests on Google Play's default\-handler requirement alone rather than on an iOS comparison (the conclusion is unchanged; only its stated basis needed correcting, and Windows has no equivalent capability either), and §2.5's accessibility settings are specified as platform\-neutral values rather than in any one framework's vocabulary. **The shared\-stack question is now settled: Compose Multiplatform, one Kotlin codebase (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** One shared adaptive UI tree serving both targets, with the platform seam confined to a single `expect`/`actual` scheduling API over two native implementations. The reasoning: every financial engine in this document — §3.9's New Month sweep and catch\-up, §5.1's FIFO walk, all four Reports, §6's Goal calculator, §7.2's amortization split — is pure platform\-independent logic, and the alternative that writes it all twice scored worst of every option considered. Compose's `CompositionLocal` architecture also happens to be the exact shape §2.5 needs: runtime theme switching, a density scale, and a text\-scale multiplier applied to every token are first\-class concepts in it rather than things built against the grain. **The one place this choice loses is footprint:** the Windows build ships a JVM, which a local\-only budgeting app pays for and gets nothing back from. **Runner\-up: Flutter**, which came within 0.025 of it and wins outright if binary size is weighted heavily — recorded because the two are close enough that a later reversal would be a real option rather than a defeat. **Two native codebases, .NET MAUI and Capacitor/Electron were all eliminated**, with margins in §13.4. Note that this decision does not reduce the platform commitment §3.9 and §3.11 describe: two native scheduling implementations exist under any stack, and on the Windows side neither Compose nor any other cross\-platform option supplies one, so a Task Scheduler entry, a WinRT toast shim and a startup entry for boot recovery are work no stack choice removes. **What the desktop looks like is settled separately, in §4.8 (Decided, 2026\-09\-02)** — adaptive reflow with the navigation, pane and dialog transforms specified, rather than a separately authored desktop shell. Sharing a UI tree is what makes that cheap, but it did not decide it.

**Five top\-level tabs:** Budget, Activity, ~~Learn~~ **Science** (renamed 2026\-10\-28, owner: a noun like the other four, and what the topics are drawn from; Italian *Scienza*; the code and this document still call it Learn), Reports, Settings — see §4 for the functional spec of each.

**Networked features (Decided, 2026\-09\-14).** Every feature in the app that can touch the network, in one place, so that "nothing leaves the device" (§4.3, §8.3) is checkable rather than a vibe:

| Feature | Trigger | Can it run in the background? |
| --- | --- | --- |
| *none* | — | — |

Statement import (§4.3) reads a file the person picked, on this device; notifications (§3.9, §3.11, §4.3, §11.5) are scheduled with the OS and carry no payload anywhere; Profiles (§8) are local. **The Android manifest omits `android.permission.INTERNET`**, so the claim is structural — an app without that permission cannot open a socket, whatever a later bug or dependency might try — and the Windows build makes no network call. A future feature that needs the network adds its row here **first**, states its trigger and its background behaviour, and only then is designed. This is the shape a sibling project uses for the same promise; the difference is that its table has rows.

## 2\. Design Philosophy (the throughline)

This section governs every layout, copy, and interaction decision elsewhere in the spec — when something elsewhere seems to conflict with it, this section wins.

### 2\.1 No valence color (Decided; bar mechanics specified 2026\-09\-02)

**The bar, the sparkline and the month columns keep their phone shape (Decided 2026\-09\-26, with the owner — closes §13.1 item 1).** Each one's height follows its own width, at the proportions it has on a 360dp phone: the bar 308 × 5 (62:1), the sparkline 306 × 64, the month columns 332 × 150 (`ChartShapes` in `ui/Tokens.kt`). On the desktop that makes the bar 7.1dp, the sparkline about 92 and the columns about 207, bounded because their width is capped by §4.8's measure. The hatch stroke and the tick stay on `radii.micro` and `strokes`, off this axis. Scored on measured widths across 108 phone and desktop contexts: keeping the literals left the desktop bar at 87:1, and scaling by the phone factor alone changed nothing above 600dp.

One accent color per theme, used identically regardless of whether a category or goal is over or under its reference point. Going past a plan or reaching\-and\-passing a goal is shown by a **tick mark \+ hatched fill segment on the same progress bar** — a pattern change, not a color change. No theme carries a separate "positive"/"warning" color pair.

**The bar's mechanics, specified (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — this parenthetical was previously the pattern's entire specification, which §14's build\-from\-spec rule can't support since the tick\+hatch is the *only* encoding of over\-reference state in the app):**

- **The bar's extent is `max(reference, actual)`, not the reference point.** The tick marks the reference wherever it falls, and the hatched segment spans reference→actual. A €200\-planned category at €300 spent therefore draws the tick at two\-thirds and hatches the final third — so *how far* past is legible. Pinning the bar to the reference and overlaying the overflow would make €210 and €600 look identical, which §2.2's "plain figures" commitment rules out.
- **Equality is not "past."** At exactly reference \= actual the tick sits at the right edge and no hatch is drawn. This matches the strict\-exceed convention §3.5 already uses for its own threshold, and keeps a person who landed exactly on plan out of a state labelled as overshooting.
- **A no\-target Goal (`target: null`, §6.3) renders no bar at all** — just the running total. There is no reference point to tick against, and drawing an unfillable bar would imply a completion condition §6.3 explicitly refuses to create.
- **§2.2's mandated plain caption states the difference and nothing else** — *"€100 past plan"* when hatched, *"€40 left of plan"* when not. No verdict, no adjective, no punctuation carrying tone. The caption is the same weight and color as any other figure on the row.

The hatch pattern is load\-bearing information, not decoration, and §2.5's low\-distraction mode must never hide it.

### 2\.2 No grading, no streaks (Decided)

Nothing in the app scores a month, a category, or a goal as a pass/fail. Reports show trends and plain figures — a Savings vs. Owed dip is drawn exactly like a rise (§5.3); a category over plan gets a plain caption and a same\-weight bar, never a verdict.

### 2\.3 Autonomy\-supportive, never blocking (Decided; copy rule scoped 2026\-09\-02)

Feasibility and sustainability checks — goal\-vs\-available\-money (§6.2), the recurring\-expense threshold (§3.5), §6.4's sustainability view, and the manual\-path "no income logged" note (§3.3) — **inform, they never block**. The person may know something the app doesn't: a raise coming, willingness to cut elsewhere.

**The closing\-language requirement is scoped to notes attached to a confirmable action (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — the earlier blanket phrasing didn't survive contact with the ambient notes added since).** Two kinds of note exist and they need different copy:

- **Notes attached to something being saved** (§6.2's and §6.4's views inside the Goal calculator; any §9.2 action that would change a real balance) end with language making the non\-blocking stance explicit — *"You can still save it as\-is."* There is something to save, so the sentence means something.
- **Ambient notes attached to no action at all** (§3.5's threshold note, permanently present on Budget → Envelopes; §3.3's "no income logged in the last X days") state a plain fact and stop. They carry no corrective instruction, no verdict, and no save\-it\-as\-is clause — appending one to a standing note about nothing in particular is incoherent, and a permanent note that reads as an instruction is a nag whatever its color.

Both kinds use the same non\-valence `feasibility-note` treatment (§2.1): the accent at reduced emphasis, never a warning color, never an icon that reads as an alert.

**"Permanently present" is satisfied by a collapsed note whose figures stay visible (Decided, 2026\-09\-03).** §3.5's threshold note is **collapsed by default**, showing the two plain figures it compares — *"€1,364 recurring · €1,450 income this month"* — with the interpretation behind a disclosure using the same accordion §4.5's Learn cards already use. This is not a weakening of the permanence rule, it is what the rule was protecting: the *fact* never leaves the screen, and what moves one tap away is the sentence about the fact. §2.2's commitment is to plain figures, and a collapsed note is more purely that than an expanded paragraph is. **The disclosure state is per\-session and deliberately not stored** — it holds no decision, so §12 gains no field for it. **This applies only to the ambient kind.** The notes inside the Goal calculator stay expanded, because §6.4 requires its frame view to be "always present… shown even when the number looks completely fine," and a feasibility read the person has to go looking for at the moment of confirming is not present in any useful sense. §3.3's "no income logged" note is also left uncollapsed: it is a single sentence with no interpretation layer to hide, so collapsing it would trade a line of fact for a line of chrome.

### 2\.4 Motivational register (Decided, 2026\-07\-18 — via `app-register-layout`; vocabulary made self\-contained 2026\-09\-02)

Formally audited rather than assumed: Sci\-Fin's `motivational_tone` is placed at **\~0.35**, well below the generic "personal finance / budget tracker" anchor of 0.75 — where the scale runs 0 (purely ambient, no encouragement of any kind) to 1 (explicit performance coaching). The generic anchor assumes routine adherence tracking; Sci\-Fin's actual purpose — per the research audit's shame/avoidance findings (audit §2.3) and Self\-Determination\-Theory findings (audit §3.5) — is closer to *shame reduction* than *adherence tracking*, the same edge case the calibration reference names explicitly for financial apps that may reach people in real distress.

**The pattern vocabulary, defined here so this document is self\-contained (added 2026\-09\-02 — §14's build\-from\-spec rule can't be met by a term whose only definition lives in an external tool).** §4.2 assigns one of these to every screen:

- **habit\-loop** — a screen returned to often, whose job is to make one small action easy and repeatable. The primary figure sits at the top with its action beside it, and where a figure is shown against a reference point, the reference is drawn as information (§2.1's tick and hatch) rather than rendered as a verdict.
- **ambient\-reflection** — a screen looked at rather than acted on. A trend or plain figure is the visual; there is no call to action.
- **card\-grid** — a screen presenting a set of peer items with no ranking between them. One element may still lead by position or size; what card\-grid forbids is ordering the peers by any measure of how well they are doing.
- **single\-column** — plain configuration. Order is functional, and any emphasis is about findability rather than importance.

**How this squares with §4.2's assignments:** habit\-loop and ambient\-reflection are the two patterns that carry tone. card\-grid and single\-column are tone\-neutral — they contain no comparison\-to\-target for a low tone to correct — which is why §4.2 can name a primary emphasis on those screens (Activity's Savings row, Settings' Appearance panel) without conflicting with this section: that is a statement about where the eye lands first, not a ranking. What §2 forbids everywhere, without exception, is **status\-board** or **scoreboard**\: any pattern that scores, ranks, or renders a figure against a target as a verdict. §2.1/§2.2 are what a correctly\-placed low tone predicts, not stylistic preferences layered on top.

### 2\.5 Accessibility as first\-class (Decided; values specified 2026\-09\-02)

Not an afterthought bolted onto a light/dark toggle — a first\-class settings surface. **Every setting below now carries the concrete values needed to build it (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Previously this section named five surfaces and specified none of them, leaving the prototype as the only source — which §14's build\-from\-spec rule forbids for values and defaults.

- **Four themes on one pair of grounds (Decided 2026\-10\-07, with the owner — supersedes the four below).** **Swiss Blue** (the default), **Daylight**, **Tallow & Tome** and **Dusk**, every one on the same grounds: light `#F4F4F2`, dark `#17181A`. They came from the visual\-craft lexicon, read against the owner's question of whether the app had verged on non\-judgement to the point of having no personality: four keywords (Serene, Health / Consumer Wellbeing, Light / Dark Academia, Swiss) were chosen, two of them obliquely, their eight named palettes rendered in the real app, and the owner kept Swiss Blue (Swiss), Daylight (Health) and Tallow & Tome (Academia). Still Water and Daylight measured as near\-twins (accent ΔE 2.5–5.2), as did Marginalia and Tallow & Tome. **Dusk** is new: `tools/palette_derive.py --score` placed a Serene palette (Still Water's lightness and chroma) in the widest hue gap the three kept accents leave, hue 334, and it scored highest of six candidates for the fourth slot, its nearest accent ΔE 20.2 away (Swiss Teal 16.3, Linen 12.1, Chalk 9.0, Marginalia 6.1, Still Water 2.5); the owner approved and named it. **How the colours are made:** each lexicon colour keeps its OKLCH hue and chroma and only lightness moves, enumerated in 0.001 steps against the floors below, the value nearest the lexicon's own winning; the other tokens derive from the theme's hue (cards and insets carry a faint tint of it, the owner's choice of where a theme's colour shows). **The accent is held to 7:1** on `bg`, `surf` and `surf2` and under its own `onAcc` label, in every theme — the owner's rule, from visual\-craft's outdoor\-legibility floor for navigation labels — because it now also draws **section titles, the headline figure and the selected tab** (decided the same day). **Daylight is adapted for phone screens** at the owner's request: its tints at 55% chroma and its accent at 80%, the margin OEM "Vivid" display modes eat. **A retired theme's stored or imported token is read as the nearest new theme by hue, never rejected:** `slate` and `tide` → Swiss Blue, `wabi` → Daylight, `mauve` → Dusk (`ThemeTokenTest`, `ImportBackupTest`). **The picker** (§11.1's first screen and Settings › Appearance) is a grid of two cards per row, each exactly the touch\-target height and never taller than 48dp, holding an accent swatch and the name, every name at one size, the largest that fits; on a 320dp phone at 180–200% text the swatch steps aside so the name stays whole (`ThemeGridTest`). Mode is unchanged: it follows the OS by default, with the in\-app override. The dark ground sits at about 9% lightness, above the 3–6% visual\-craft suggests for OLED panels; the owner fixed it, and it is recorded here rather than moved.
- **4 color themes × 2 modes** — ~~Slate/Default, Mauve/Delicate, Wabi/Warm\-weathered, Tide/Glacial.~~ **Renamed from *Wabi Sabi* and *Tidal Keep* (Decided, 2026\-09\-14)** — measured on §11.1's theme picker, where a card has 52–61px for its name across the 320–360dp phone widths: both two\-word names are 61–64px and wrap in every frame, every one\-word name of up to seven letters fits with room. ~~The stored tokens are `slate`, `mauve`, `wabi`, `tide`;~~ the prototype's earlier `wabisabi`/`tidalkeep` are read as legacy values and rewritten on load. Each theme carries exactly one accent, no valence pair. **Mode** means light/dark, and **follows the OS setting by default**, with an explicit in\-app override (system / light / dark). ~~**Default theme: Slate**~~ **Default theme: Swiss Blue since 2026\-10\-07** — which is what §11.1's step 1 means when it says Continue simply keeps whatever theme is already selected.
  - **The eight palettes are transcribed in full below (2026\-09\-02), and the transcription changed the design rather than merely recording it.** Measuring them first found two token pairs failing their contrast requirement in all eight palettes; the remedy is specified under *The muted\-text and border remedy* below, and the values that follow it are post\-remedy. This closes §14's build\-from\-spec gap: nothing about the visual system now requires reading `sci-fin-prototype.html`.
- **The app icon (Decided, 2026\-09\-15).** The person's own lettering — an S and an F set in Metamorphous (SIL OFL), the F's stem run into the S's lower bowl, drawn in Affinity — in white on the Slate accent, and the same under every theme: the icon is the app's mark, not the chosen palette's. Four marks in this section's register were mocked up first (`design/icon-options.html`: §2.1's bar with its tick and hatch, a stroked SF, an open ring, a vessel) and the lettering was shown beside them white\-on\-Slate and Slate\-on\-light; the person chose their own, on the accent. The source is ~~`design/icon/sci-fin-icon.svg`~~ `design/icon/lettering-paths.svg` — the text outlined by Inkscape, so no font ships — and `tools/build_icon.py` generates everything from it, `sci-fin-icon.svg` included (corrected 2026\-09\-25: the script reads the one and writes the other, so editing the named file changed nothing; ~~its bounding box is still typed in from an Inkscape query rather than read~~ its bounding box is read from `inkscape --query-all` on every run, since later the same day): the Android adaptive icon (a vector foreground with a 0.6dp thickening, the colour background, and a monochrome layer for Android 13's themed icons; the mark spans 55 of the 66dp safe zone) and, for Windows, a 16–256px ladder whose small frames carry a size\-dependent outward stroke so the face's hairlines survive 16 and 24px — the MSI's `.ico` and the running window's `iconImages`, set on the frame directly because Compose's `Window(icon=)` paints one 192px image and leaves Windows to downscale it into a smudge. Verified on the OnePlus's launcher and on the Windows title bar and taskbar.
- **Text follows the operating system's text\-size setting, on both platforms (Decided, 2026\-09\-15).** Android's font scale already applies, because the ramp is expressed in `sp`; Windows' Settings › Accessibility › Text size (`TextScaleFactor`, 100–225%) is not read by Compose Desktop and the window reads it itself, so the two apps meet the same setting the person set for everything else. ~~**Not built, found 2026\-09\-26:**~~ **Built 2026\-09\-26, the day it was found unbuilt (with the owner):** Compose Desktop 1.12 fixes `fontScale` at 1 (`LayoutConfiguration.desktop.kt`, JetBrains CMP\-6391), so the desktop host reads `TextScaleFactor` once at launch and applies it as the window's font scale (`parseTextScaleFactor`, `TextScaleTest`); a change made while the app is open takes effect on the next launch. ~~The in\-app setting below is kept, with a fifth value **System** as its default — the OS value — and sm / md / lg / xl as absolute overrides that replace it rather than multiply it.~~ **Superseded 2026\-09\-23 (Decided): the in\-app text\-size setting is removed altogether, and the OS value is the only one.** One multiplier, set in one place, the place a person has already set it for everything else on the device. What this costs is stated rather than discovered: the OS setting is system\-wide on both platforms, so there is no longer any way to make Sci\-Fin's text larger than the rest of the device and nothing else — a real reduction in what §2.5 offers, accepted deliberately in exchange for a setting that cannot disagree with the system. A backup (§4.7) carrying the old `scale` value is read and ignored, which needs no migration. Built in the scale pass (Stage 3, step 8).
- ~~**Text scale**\: sm / md / lg / xl~~ **— retired as a setting 2026\-09\-23; the ramp and its ratios below are unchanged, and the OS factor is what now multiplies the base.** Originally: sm / md / lg / xl \= **0\.875× / 1× / 1.25× / 1.5×** of a **16px base** (14 / 16 / 20 / 24px), applied to every text token proportionally rather than to body text alone. The range is deliberately wider than a typographic scale would suggest — xl exists to be genuinely usable, not to be a slightly larger md. **What "every text token" means is stated rather than implied (specified 2026\-09\-03):** the ramp is the seven tokens in *The type ramp* below, each a fixed ratio of the base, and the setting multiplies **the base only** — never the individual tokens. That is the whole mechanism by which the ramp stays proportional at all four settings, and it is the part the prototype had hand\-tuned rather than multiplied (§14).
- **Density**\: comfortable / compact \= base spacing unit **`u` \= 8px / 6px**, applied to vertical rhythm, row padding, and gaps. **The two quantities derived from it are `pad` \= 1\.75u and `gap` \= 1u (specified 2026\-09\-03)** — naming a base unit and stopping there did not tell an implementer what to do with it, and left the prototype free to use two unrelated numbers (§14). **Touch and click targets never shrink with density** — they stay at the platform minimum (48dp on Android, 40px on Windows) at every density and text scale, since a compact layout that becomes harder to hit is an accessibility regression wearing an accessibility label. **How density meets the screen factor (Decided, 2026\-09\-23):** the two multiply, and `u` is floored at **6dp** — without the floor, compact on a 320dp phone resolves to 5\.3dp, a combination the app has never been looked at in. Both settings stay meaningful; neither can compound the other into something cramped.
- **Reduce motion**\: disables all interface transitions and animations; state changes apply instantly. No motion is load\-bearing anywhere, so nothing is lost.
- **Low\-distraction mode**\: hides purely decorative detail — background gradients and textures, entity icons (§12's `icon` fields), and non\-essential dividers. **It never hides any figure, any caption, the `feasibility-note` treatment (§2.3), or the tick \+ hatched\-fill pattern (§2.1, drawn in `txt` and `acc` — never `txt3`)** — the hatch is the only non\-color encoding of over\-reference state in the entire app, so hiding it would silently remove information rather than reduce noise.

**Keyboard and assistive operability (Decided, 2026\-09\-14).** Until this date the five settings above were the whole of this section, and the prototype's rows, switches and checkboxes were plain elements with no focus order at all — on the Windows layout §4.8 ships, the app could not be driven from a keyboard, which is an accessibility regression wearing a first\-class label. The rule, harvested from a sibling project and applied centrally rather than control by control:

- **Every actionable element is reachable by Tab and activated by Enter or Space,** with the same handler and the same value a tap would send. Form fields are left to the platform: Enter in a field never fires the field's own action a second time, and Space in a text field is a space.
- **Escape is Cancel.** It closes whatever is open — the quick\-add sheet, a confirmation, an editor, a Settings spoke — and writes nothing, which is exactly what every Cancel in the app already does (§3.6). There is no path from Escape that saves.
- **Toggles say what they are.** A switch exposes `switch` and its checked state; a tick box exposes `checkbox`; everything else actionable is a `button`. Assistive technology therefore reads "New Month, switch, on" rather than "group".
- **One focus ring, `:focus\-visible` only.** The ring is `acc` at the control's own radius, so a tap never paints one and a keyboard always does; `acc` already clears 3:1 against `surf` and `bg` in all eight palettes (the token tables below), which is what lets one rule serve every control without a second measured colour.
- **Focus survives a re\-render.** A keyboard\-driven toggle rebuilds the element under the caret; the replacement is found by the same action and value, so Space on a switch leaves the focus on that switch rather than dropping it to the top of the screen.
- **Small controls keep their size and grow their hit area.** Anything drawn below the platform minimum (§12's table: 48 dp / 40 px) carries a centred, transparent box ~~of **44 px**~~ **the size of that minimum** (corrected 2026\-09\-25 with §13.1 item 10: 44 sat below the floor it serves, and the build already used the minimum) that moves nothing and repaints nothing — the icon button still looks 30 px and behaves like ~~44~~ 48. **The round icon button is a 30dp circle with a 20dp glyph (Decided 2026\-09\-26, with the owner)** — 3.75u and 2.5u, so both scale with the screen. The build had drawn the whole 48dp target as the circle around a 14dp glyph, which set the height of every card holding one; the circle is now laid out at its drawn size and the hit area grows leftward to the platform minimum, as §14's 2026\-09\-25 entry and the prototype's `.qa-btn::after` do. Beside another round button, the left one's area is on top, so every drawn circle answers for its own button (`RoundButtonTest`).

**The token set (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Eleven tokens per mode, twenty\-two per theme, eighty\-eight across the app. Every one of them is required by something; none is decorative slack.

| Token | Role |
| --- | --- |
| `bg` | the screen behind everything |
| `surf` | cards, rows, sheets — the raised plane |
| `surf2` | insets within a surface: segmented controls, progress tracks, info notes |
| `bord` | hairline separation where the boundary is decorative and the surface delta already reads |
| `bordStrong` | **new 2026\-09\-02** — any boundary carrying meaning on its own: input fields, the tab bar's top edge, a card edge where `surf` and `bg` are close |
| `txt` | primary text, and the over\-reference tick (§2.1) |
| `txt2` | every secondary or supporting text that still carries information |
| `txt3` | **decorative and inactive only** — see the remedy below |
| `acc` | the single accent: fills, selected state, the hatch |
| `accH` | that accent's hover/pressed variant |
| `onAcc` | text or glyph sitting on an accent fill |

**The muted\-text and border remedy (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Transcribing the palettes required measuring them, and measuring them found that `txt3`/`bg` ran 2.44:1 – 3.43:1 and `bord`/`surf` ran 1.27:1 – 1.56:1 — failing in every one of the eight, against 4.5:1 for text and 3:1 for a non\-text boundary. `txt3` was not carrying decoration: it held section labels, a Recurring Expense's cadence line, Learn's research\-source citations, inactive tab labels, chart axis labels, and the over\-reference **tick mark** that §2.1 and §2.5 both name as the only non\-color encoding of state in the entire app. Darkening `txt3` to 4.5:1 would have landed it on top of `txt2` and bought a third text tier that was really a second. So the tier was rescoped instead:

- **Everything informational that was `txt3` becomes `txt2`.** Section labels, cadence lines, source citations, inactive tab labels, chart axis labels, disclosure chevrons, onboarding sub\-copy. `txt2` clears 4.5:1 against both `bg` and `surf` in all eight palettes, so this is a straight promotion with no new value needed.
- **`txt3` survives for decorative and inactive use only** — a disabled control, a glyph that merely accompanies text already stating the same thing. Its values are unchanged and it does **not** meet 4.5:1; that is a stated exemption resting on WCAG's own carve\-out for inactive components and pure decoration, and it is the reason `txt3` may never again carry a fact. If a value can only be read from a `txt3` element, that is a bug against this section.
- **The tick mark leaves `txt3` entirely and is drawn in `txt`, at 2px rather than 1px.** It clears 9.89:1 – 12.63:1 against the progress track (`surf2`) in every palette. Drawing the app's sole non\-color accessibility affordance as a 1px hairline at 2.44:1 contradicted the guarantee §2.1 makes.
- **`bord` splits in two rather than darkening wholesale.** `bord` keeps its current value for decorative hairlines, exempt as decoration; `bordStrong` is new, meets ≥3:1 against **both** `surf` and `bg` in all eight palettes, and is required wherever a boundary is the only thing communicating a control's extent. Darkening every border to 3:1 would have made the app read as a wireframe and cost the quiet register §2.4 asks for. This reuses the essential/non\-essential divider distinction low\-distraction mode already draws.

**Measured contrast (2026\-09\-02; `txt2`/`surf2` added and three light `txt2` values darkened, Decided 2026\-09\-15).** Every pair the app actually composites. Text pairs are held to 4.5:1, non\-text boundaries to 3:1; the lowest value anywhere in this table is 3.05. The 2026\-09\-15 device critique re\-measured the table from `Tokens.kt` and found the one ground it had never listed: `txt2` on `surf2`, where `InlineLearn`, the `InfoNote` and the disclosure body actually sit, measured 4.44 (Slate), 4.36 (Mauve) and 4.11 (Wabi) in light mode. The three light `txt2` values were darkened by the smallest step that clears 4.5 there (`#6B6B70`→`#66666B`, `#75636A`→`#6F5D64`, `#6E6A5C`→`#666254`); every other cell is unchanged, and `TokenContrastTest` now computes this table from the tokens rather than trusting the transcription. The same critique found two research\-source citations still drawn in `txt3` (Learn topics and the inline\-learn footer), against the remedy above; both are `txt2` now and the test forbids a `Text` in `txt3` outside the two placeholder sites.

| Palette | `txt`/`bg` | `txt`/`surf` | `txt`/`surf2` | `txt2`/`bg` | `txt2`/`surf` | `txt2`/`surf2` | `acc`/`bg` | `acc`/`surf` | `acc`/`surf2` | `onAcc`/`acc` | `bordStrong`/`surf` | `bordStrong`/`bg` |

| Daylight Light | 14.63 | 15.28 | 13.37 | 5.47 | 5.71 | 5.00 | 7.71 | 8.05 | 7.05 | 8.49 | 4.54 | 4.34 |
| Daylight Dark | 14.37 | 12.86 | 11.23 | 6.16 | 5.52 | 4.82 | 9.01 | 8.06 | 7.04 | 9.01 | 3.33 | 3.72 |
| Tallow & Tome Light | 16.07 | 16.70 | 14.54 | 5.49 | 5.70 | 4.97 | 8.66 | 9.00 | 7.84 | 9.54 | 4.63 | 4.45 |

| Swiss Blue Dark | 15.84 | 14.29 | 12.30 | 6.22 | 5.62 | 4.83 | 9.02 | 8.14 | 7.00 | 9.02 | 3.32 | 3.68 |
| Dusk Light | 11.47 | 11.86 | 10.36 | 5.52 | 5.70 | 4.98 | 7.79 | 8.06 | 7.04 | 8.58 | 4.63 | 4.47 |

Two pairs are deliberately absent and accounted for rather than omitted. **`txt3` against anything** is exempt by the remedy above and ranges 2.44:1 – 3.43:1; it is recorded here so a later reader does not rediscover it as a defect. **The hatch** (§2.1) is not a palette entry — it is `acc` at 85% opacity in a `repeating-linear-gradient(135deg, acc 0 3px, transparent 3px 6px)` over `surf2`, which measures 3.30:1 – 4.46:1 against that track across the eight and so clears the 3:1 a non\-text pattern requires. `accH` is a pressed/hover variant of `acc` and inherits its pairing.

**The geometry system: type ramp, spacing, radii and strokes (Decided, 2026\-09\-03).** Colour was the only part of the visual system this section carried values for. Everything with a *size* in it — the type ramp, every spacing value, every corner radius, every stroke weight — still lived only in `sci-fin-prototype.html`, which is the same build\-from\-spec gap the palettes had (§14) wearing different clothes. It is closed the same way: by transcribing what the prototype actually does, correcting it where it turned out not to be a system, and stating the result as **proportions rather than pixel lists**, so one set of rules resolves at any screen size instead of one hardcoded table per screen class.

**Everything below scales, and there are exactly three axes it scales on.** They are independent, they multiply, and nothing may introduce a fourth.

| Axis | Set by | Multiplies |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Screen** — `uiScale` | the platform: size class and OS display scaling. Never a user\-facing control | every geometry unit, and the type base |
| **Density** — comfortable / compact | the person (this section) | the spacing unit `u` only |
| ~~**Text scale** — sm / md / lg / xl~~ **Text scale — the platform's own font size** (the in\-app setting withdrawn 2026\-09\-23, removed from the app 2026\-09\-25, schema 5) | ~~the person (this section)~~ the OS | the type base only |

~~`uiScale` is **1 on a phone** — the ≤600dp class, which is what the prototype's 360×756 frame is — and rises on the wider classes §4.8's reflow already defines: **1 / 1\.125 / 1\.25** for phone / tablet / desktop.~~ **Continuous, not stepped (Decided, 2026\-09\-15):** `uiScale` is the window's width divided by the 360dp reference (the onboarding sheet's own factor, which divided by 332 while its comment said 360, now divides by 360 — ~~the sheet's own scale function, tested~~ — since 2026\-09\-25 the sheet follows §2.5's phone rule itself through `screenScale()`, and its own capped function is deleted (Decided, with the owner: on a 412dp phone it had stayed at 1.0 while the screens behind it read 1.14)), recomputed on every resize, so that every measurement in the app is proportional to the screen or window it is on — phone and desktop alike, as the person asked for it, and as §11.1's sheet already does. The three steps above are withdrawn: a 1440px window is not the same size as an 1100px one and should not look it. ~~The factor is clamped below at 1 (a 320dp phone keeps the reference sizes and wraps, §2.5) and above at a ceiling~~ **On a phone the factor is floored at 0\.85 with no ceiling (next paragraph, 2026\-09\-23); on the desktop it is capped at a ceiling** (struck and reworded 2026\-09\-25) whose value is to be confirmed on the scale pass's renders rather than asserted here. **The rule this makes enforceable:** no `dp` literal anywhere under `ui/` — the 2026\-09\-15 audit found nineteen files carrying them (icon sizes, the §2.1 bar's height and tick, small paddings), every one a defect against the sentence that follows, and the scale pass removes them. A platform display\-scaling factor multiplies on top of that rather than replacing it. **No measurement anywhere in the app is a bare pixel constant**; every one resolves from a unit below. That property is what lets a single specification fit a 360dp phone and a 1440px desktop window without a second set of numbers, and it is why §4.8 can be built from this document even though the prototype is phone\-only.

**The phone's half of the rule, settled 2026\-09\-23 (Decided); the desktop's half waits on §4.8's revisit.** Below 600dp there is no navigation rail and no second pane, so the window and the column a person reads are the same number and the factor is unambiguous: **width ÷ 360dp, floored at 0\.85 and with no ceiling** — the phone range runs 0\.89 at 320dp through 1\.00 at 360dp to about 1\.33 on a 480dp phone, narrow enough that a ceiling would buy nothing. **It multiplies the spacing unit `u` (and therefore `pad` and `gap`) and icon and glyph sizes, and nothing else.** Corner radii and stroke weights are explicitly *not* on this axis any more, which supersedes the two paragraphs below that put them there: a corner and a hairline are properties of the shape, and a 0\.5px hairline multiplied by 0\.89 is a hairline that may not paint at all. Touch and click targets are unaffected, as they are by every other axis. **Why the desktop is not settled here:** the reference itself is contested above 600dp — the window is 1100dp but §4.8 splits it into an 88dp rail, a 368dp list column and a 644dp detail column, so "the width" is three different numbers — and §4.8 is being reopened (below), which may change what those columns are. **It did: the list and detail columns went with rule 2 on 2026\-09\-24 (note added 2026\-09\-25).**

**The frame owns the system bars (Decided, 2026\-09\-15).** On Android the palette's `bg` runs under the status and navigation bars and the content keeps clear of them by the safe\-drawing insets; the bar glyphs follow the app's own light/dark resolution, not the OS theme. Until this the status bar was a black band over a light page and the navigation bar a white one under a dark page — the first and last 7 % of every screen contradicting the palette (2026\-09\-15 device critique). Host configuration in `MainActivity`, checked on the device rather than by a test.

**The type system: four sizes, one per job, and two weights (Decided 2026\-09\-26, with the owner — supersedes the seven\-token ramp below).** The owner's brief: *too many text sizes and weights in the mix*, and *one or more of the jobs should inherit the device's text size and the others should be sized proportionally*. So **body is the device's text size**, and every other job is a fixed ratio of it on a perfect fourth (1.333): **caption 0.75** (12px at the default size) — captions, chart labels, sources, section labels (bold, uppercase — **section titles stay in capitals (Decided 2026\-09\-26, with the owner)**, so a title such as INCOME reads as a different kind of text from the sentence\-case labels and rows beneath it) and the navigation labels; **body 1** (16px) — everything read as text, bold where a row names something; **title 1.333** (21.3px) — a screen's, page's or sheet's title and a result that heads a card; **figure 1.777** (28.4px) — the one headline amount or count a view is about. **Weights: Regular and Bold only.** In `sp`, so the device's text size multiplies all four and the ratios hold (`typeRamp()` in `ui/Tokens.kt`; `TypeSystemTest` fails on any fifth size, third weight or literal `sp`). **How it was chosen:** a census of the UI code found 23 size\+weight styles over 7 sizes 1.077 apart and 4 weights, with one job drawn up to three ways (screen titles in `lg` Bold, `xl` Bold and `xl` ExtraBold). Five candidates were rendered in the real app and scored with `designkit`'s own hierarchy arithmetic against a census of Material 3, iOS Dynamic Type and three open\-source finance apps (Ivy Wallet, Cashew, Actual): the old ramp 0.66, a major third 0.85, **this perfect fourth 0.93**, Material 3's ratios 0.54 and iOS's 0.59 — the platforms' own ratios carry their many 11–15px steps, which is the crowding being removed. Both platforms keep their default set to two weights; the finance apps without a held scale spread to 24–46 styles in use.

~~**The type ramp.** Seven tokens, given as ratios of the base.~~ *(Superseded 2026\-09\-26 by the paragraph above; kept as the trail.)* The ratios are the prototype's own ramp — 11 / 12 / 13 / 14 / 16 / 18 / 22 — **rebased from its 13px base onto this section's 16px** (2026\-09\-03). The prototype's `base` was a mockup\-scale artifact: 360×756 CSS pixels stands in for a 360dp phone, where 16px is what body text should actually render at, so 13px was drawing the whole app about 19% small. The ramp shape is adopted; its base is corrected. The setting multiplies the base, so all four columns are one multiplication apart:

| Token | Ratio | sm (14px) | md (16px) | lg (20px) | xl (24px) | Carries |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `xs` | 0\.846 | 11\.8 | 13\.5 | 16\.9 | 20\.3 | captions, cadence lines, badges, tab labels, source citations |
| `sm` | 0\.923 | 12\.9 | 14\.8 | 18\.5 | 22\.2 | section labels, field labels, chips, note bodies |
| `base` | 1 | 14 | 16 | 20 | 24 | row titles and body text — the anchor |
| `md` | 1\.077 | 15\.1 | 17\.2 | 21\.5 | 25\.8 | buttons, input fields, onboarding body |
| `lg` | 1\.231 | 17\.2 | 19\.7 | 24\.6 | 29\.5 | the brand lockup, screen sub\-headings |
| `xl` | 1\.385 | 19\.4 | 22\.2 | 27\.7 | 33\.2 | screen titles, headline figures, sheet titles |
| `2xl` | 1\.692 | 23\.7 | 27\.1 | 33\.8 | 40\.6 | onboarding headlines |

**What the ramp requires of layout (Decided, 2026\-09\-04).** A larger type setting is only genuinely usable if the layout gives the text somewhere to go, and the failure is silent: a swept audit at all four settings found labels being ellipsised away and text crossing its own card border, on screens that looked correct at md. Three rules, each learned from a defect rather than assumed:

- **A row's label wraps; it is never truncated.** Where a name sits beside a figure or a state — a Recurring Expenses summary, an Account row, a Settings row — the label is the half a reader needs to identify the row, and it is the half a single flex line takes from. *"6 recurring exp…"* and *"Car Loan"* clipped mid\-word were both real at xl. The label takes a second line instead.
- **Wrapping the row is the wrong fix, and was tried first.** Flex breaks lines on each item's *base* size, before any shrinking is applied, so a wrappable row drops its right\-hand control onto its own line while there is still room beside the label — five of six Appearance toggles did exactly that at the default setting. Wrap the text, keep the row one line.
- **Any label that may break must use `break-word`, never `anywhere`.** `anywhere` participates in intrinsic min\-content sizing, so a flex label is handed a one\-character minimum and wraps where it plainly fits — "Income" arrived on two lines. `break-word` breaks only when a line would actually overflow.

A grid or flex item also has to be told it may shrink (`min-width: 0`); the default `auto` refuses to go below the longest word, which is what pushed the theme cards past the screen and their kind labels past their own borders at every setting, not just xl. **The bar this section holds layout to: at every text scale, no text is ellipsised away and no text leaves the card it belongs to.**

**Spacing.** One unit, two derived quantities, and every other spacing value in the app is a multiple of `gap`:

| Quantity | Formula | comfortable | compact |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `u` — the base unit | — | 8px | 6px |
| `pad` — screen and sheet side padding, card interior | 1\.75u | 14 | 10\.5 |
| `gap` — the rhythm between stacked elements | 1u | 8 | 6 |

Three of those four values are what the prototype already used. **Comfortable `gap` moves 10px → 8px** so that the one stated unit actually produces them. The alternative — restating the formulas as 1\.75u / 1\.25u to preserve the 10 — was rejected because it would make this section describe an accident instead of specifying a rhythm, and the 2px is worth less than the formula.

**Corner radii.** Seven steps, as multiples of a radius unit `ru` \= ~~8px × `uiScale`~~ 8dp (corrected 2026\-09\-25, per the correction that follows). ~~**Radii scale on the screen axis only — never with density or text scale.**~~ **Radii scale on no axis at all (corrected 2026\-09\-23):** they are fixed dp, for the reason this paragraph already gives — a corner is a property of the shape rather than of the layout — which turns out to argue against the screen axis as much as against density. A corner is a property of the shape rather than of the layout, and shrinking every corner by a quarter because someone chose compact changes the app's character in exchange for no space at all. The prototype's eleven distinct radii (3 / 6 / 8 / 10 / 11 / 12 / 13 / 14 / 16 / 18 / 22) are rationalized to seven: four of them sat 1–2px apart doing a single job, which is not a system and cannot be stated as a proportion of anything.

| Step | Formula | px at `uiScale` 1 | Applies to | Rationalized from |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `r-micro` | 0\.375ru | 3 | progress tracks and fills, chart bars, legend swatches | 3 |
| `r-tiny` | 0\.75ru | 6 | checkboxes and other small square controls | 6 |
| `r-sm` | 1ru | 8 | the selected pill inside a segmented control, small insets | 8, 10 |
| `r-md` | 1\.5ru | 12 | every control (button, input, currency field) and every inset within a surface (info note, inline Learn, feasibility note, segmented track) | 11, 12 |
| `r-lg` | 1\.75ru | 14 | the raised plane — cards, rows, placeholder cards, tutorial bubbles | 13, 14 |
| `r-xl` | 2ru | 16 | dialogs, and the tutorial's highlight ring | 16, 18 |
| `r-sheet` | 2\.75ru | 22 | the bottom sheet's top corners | 22 |

**Pill (`999px`) and circle (`50%`) are shapes, not radii,** and sit outside the ladder deliberately — they resolve from the element's own height, so they already scale with everything above them.

**Stroke weights.** Three, plus the sparkline's `line` (2026\-10\-10). They carry meaning, so they are named for what they mean rather than for how thick they are. **The `× uiScale` in the table below is withdrawn (corrected 2026\-09\-23):** a stroke that carries meaning has to paint, and the hairline is already at the limit of what does; these three are fixed dp.

| Stroke | Width | Drawn in | Where |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| hairline | 0\.5px × `uiScale` | `bord` | decorative separation only — the Learn card's internal divider, and anywhere the `surf`/`bg` delta already reads |
| control | 1\.5px × `uiScale` | `bordStrong` | any boundary carrying meaning on its own: inputs, currency fields, chips, theme cards, checkboxes, the tab bar's top edge, and the dashed "\+ New" affordances whose entire extent *is* the border |
| emphasis | 2px × `uiScale` | `txt` for the tick, `acc` for the ring | the two marks drawn *over* content to say "look here": §2.1's over\-reference tick — the app's only non\-colour encoding of state (see the remedy above) — and §11.6's tutorial highlight ring. They share a weight because they share a job, which is why this is the heaviest of the three |
| line | 2\.5px | `acc` | the Reports sparkline's one stroke — a drawn series, not a boundary or a mark, so it sits outside the three above (token added 2026\-10\-10; it had been a literal) |

**One token sits below the ramp, for navigation labels only (added 2026\-09\-04, from building it; since 2026\-09\-26 it is simply the caption size, 0.75×, which the type system above made a job of its own).** `fs-2xs` \= **0\.75× the base** — 12px at md, which is Material's `label-medium`, the size a navigation bar's labels are actually specified at. The bar had been drawing them at `fs-xs` (0\.846×), one step too large for the role, and five labels one step too large do not fit a fifth of a bar: they truncated from the lg setting upward. It multiplies off the base like every other token, so all three axes still apply. **The one combination it does not rescue is the 360dp compact floor at the xl text scale**, where a fifth of the bar is 66px and the widest label needs 72px; no token choice closes that, since the arithmetic caps the label near 16\.6px where xl asks for 18. At that single setting the icon carries the meaning and the label truncates — the trade every platform's navigation bar makes at the same point, recorded here rather than left as an unexplained exception to the bar below.

**The reference device is not the compact floor (clarified 2026\-09\-04).** §4.8's width classes start at 360dp because that is the *minimum* a compact layout must survive, and it is the width to test against — not the width to design on. A current handset is 393–412dp (iPhone 15 393pt, Pixel 8 412dp, Galaxy S24 384dp), and correct phone typography rendered on a screen a size-and-a-half too small reads as oversized type rather than as an undersized screen. This is not a spec value — no layout rule depends on a device size — but it is why the prototype's 360×756 frame made a Material-accurate ramp look heavy, and why it now previews at 412×870 with the 360 floor kept as its own reviewable size.

~~**Strokes scale on the screen axis only, like radii,**~~ **Strokes are fixed dp like radii (2026\-09\-23; corrected 2026\-09\-25) and floor at one device pixel** — a hairline that rounds to zero on a low\-density display silently deletes a boundary. They deliberately do **not** grow with text scale: a border's job is to mark an edge crisply, and a control border that thickens to 2\.25px at xl reads as a heavier, busier interface rather than a more legible one, which is the opposite of what that setting is for. Legibility at xl is the type ramp's job, and the ramp already does it.

**Touch and click targets.** ~~`48px × max(1, uiScale)` on Android and `40px × max(1, uiScale)` on Windows~~ **48dp on Android and 40px on Windows, the same at every screen width (Decided 2026\-09\-25, with the owner — closes §13.1 item 10).** A dp is already about the same physical size on every screen, and a finger does not grow with the screen, so the phone rule's *unaffected* is the rule and the width multiplier is struck. A floor, never a ceiling, and never multiplied by density, per the Density bullet above. Any control whose intrinsic size from the units above lands below the floor is padded up to it rather than being allowed to shrink.

**Three things that never wrap (Decided, 2026\-09\-15).** The text\-size setting goes to 1.5× on a 360dp phone, and the device critique found the layout breaking there in three ways, each now a rule with a test (`WrapTest`): **an amount is one token** — it is set with `softWrap` off and overflows its column rather than splitting ("€1," on one line and "095" on the next is a different number); **the tab bar's five labels share one size**, the largest half\-point step from `xs` down to 9sp at which the widest label fits its cell, so the bar never shows three small labels beside two large ones and never breaks a word; **a settings row gives its label a floor of 18u** and, when the control would take that away, puts the control under the label instead of beside it. A learn link's arrow is joined to its last word with a no\-break space for the same reason. **Extended 2026\-09\-26, when body became the device's text size:** at 150% a settings row read *"Appeara nce & ac cessibilit y"*, an account *"Car Loa n"* and Reports' switch *"Spendin g"*. So **a row with something on its right keeps room for its label's longest word** and puts the right\-hand part under the label when it cannot (`BesideOrUnder`, used by navigation and account rows), and **a segmented switch follows the tab bar's rule** — one size for every option, the largest that fits, never wrapping.

**Small settlements of the same day (Decided, 2026\-09\-15).** The four text\-size options each render their **A** at their own scale (13.5px × 0.875 / 1 / 1.25 / 1.5), so the control previews what it sets. The Material date picker is **pinned to English** — its month grid through the state's locale and, on Android, its headline and buttons through an English configuration for the dialog subtree — until the app itself is localised; the phone's Italian calendar under an English app was one dialog in two languages. §3.5's recurring disclosure sits **directly under the recurring summary row** it explains, inside that section, rather than between Savings and Income.

**A link is underlined (Decided, 2026\-09\-15).** Slate's `acc` is one step from its `txt2` — the device critique measured the default palette at a mean on\-screen chroma of 0.2, and its learn links read as bold grey text. Slate stays quiet, as designed; the link therefore carries a cue that is not colour, the underline, in every palette. This is the second non\-colour encoding in the app after §2.1's tick.

~~All five settings are per\-Profile once §8 is built, not device\-level (§8.4).~~ **The four settings left — theme and mode, density, reduced motion, low\-distraction — are per\-Profile, not device\-level (§8.4); text size is the OS's (removed from the app 2026\-09\-25, schema 5).**

### 2\.6 Research grounding (Decided, summarized from the audit)

Every major structural decision below traces to a specific research finding in `sci-fin-research-audit.md`\:

- **Mental accounting** (Thaler) — money isn't experienced as fungible; this is *why* Categories/Goals/earmarking work at all, not a bias to fight (audit §2.1, §3.3).
- **Ostrich effect / financial shame** (Gladstone et al.) — red/negative styling and warning\-only feedback drive avoidance, which worsens outcomes; this is why §2.1–§2.2 exist (audit §2.3).
- **Future self\-continuity** (Hershfield) — naming a goal concretely makes the person it's for feel more real, which measurably increases saving (audit §2.4) — this is why Goals ask what they're *for*, not just a number.
- **Implementation intentions** (Gollwitzer) — concrete, revisitable plans beat vague intentions, but rigid plans misfire if circumstances change (audit §3.1) — informs why every plan in the app stays editable (§3.6) rather than becoming a locked commitment.
- **Automation and decision fatigue** (audit §3.2) — every repeated decision a budgeting tool demands is a place people drop out, and the highest\-leverage fix is to stop asking rather than to ask more nicely. **Added to this list 2026\-09\-02** — §3.9 has cited `audit §3.2` as its grounding since it was written, but this section never carried the bullet, leaving a live citation pointing at nothing. It is the direct basis for Categories' `planned` carrying forward automatically rather than being re\-asked each month (§3.9), for Recurring Expenses sweeping on schedule rather than being logged by hand (§3.11), and for auto\-deposit existing at all (§3.3).
- **Earmarking & partitioning** (Soman & Cheema) — visually separating labeled money increases saving beyond labeling alone, but can also make people protect a label even through a genuine emergency (audit §3.3) — this is why every earmarked amount (Goals, Categories) stays freely editable rather than guilt\-gated.
- **Self\-Determination Theory** (applied to personal finance) — autonomous, chosen financial motivation outperforms externally\-pressured motivation on every measured outcome (audit §3.5) — the direct basis for §2.3.
- **CFPB Financial Well\-Being framework** — well\-being is a felt sense of security and freedom of choice, not adherence to a plan (audit §3.7) — the direct basis for the Buffer/Age\-of\-Money report being the app's default view (§5.1) rather than a spending breakdown.
- **ADHD/autism\-specific findings** — automation and externalized reminders are the highest\-leverage intervention for ADHD; predictability and low\-distraction modes matter most for autism (audit §4.1–§4.2) — informs quick\-add's role (§3.6) and the low\-distraction setting (§2.5).

### 2\.7 Languages (Decided with the owner 2026\-10\-26; built in three steps, Italian offered from step 3)

**English and Italian first**, the system built so that a further language is a catalogue, not code.

- **Which language:** the device's, with English as the fallback for any language not shipped, **overridable per Profile in Appearance** — a Profile carries its language as it carries its theme (§8.4). Before a Profile exists (§11.1's first screen) and on the PIN gate (§4.7), the device's language is used.
- **The palette names stay in English in every language** (*Swiss Blue*, *Daylight*, *Tallow & Tome*, *Dusk*; owner, 2026\-10\-28): they are names, not descriptions.
- **Italian is written in the informal *tu*** — the usual register for personal\-finance apps and the closest to the English tone (§2.4).
- **Dates and amounts take the language's own style:** English `Nov 13` and `€1,450.50`; Italian `13 nov` and `1.450,50 €`. The currency setting still chooses the symbol.
- **Text the app itself writes into the data** — ledger labels such as *Payday*, *Auto\-deposit*, *Restored from the Trash* — is stored in English and **shown in the active language**, so switching language changes old rows too; what the person typed stays as typed.
- **Every translation is reviewed by the owner before it ships**, the Learn topics as their own batch: a translation can overstate a finding the English was careful to narrow (§4.5).
- **Mechanism:** the English text is the key. Every string the person reads passes through `tr`, `trf` (numbered placeholders, which a translation may reorder) or `trp` (one and other forms) in `i18n/I18n.kt`; each other language is a catalogue of English → its text. `LocalisationTest` reads every key from the source, fails the build if a shipped catalogue lacks one or if screen text reaches a component without `tr`, and pins both languages' formats. **Where English uses one word in two senses** that another language tells apart — *Log* the verb (*Registra*) and the noun (*Registro*) — the second sense goes through `trc`, keyed `sense::English` (2026\-10\-28).
- **Notifications are written in the Profile's language when they are scheduled, not when they appear** (2026\-10\-28). The OS keeps the text, and the background run that delivers or re\-arms them may start the app without opening a screen: it reads the language from the Profile before composing anything. Android's New Month ping carries its text with it like the due\-date reminders; changing the language re\-arms New Month and renames Android's notification channels.
- **Delivered in three PRs:** (1) every string routed through `tr` and the formats made language\-aware, with no visible change in English; (2) the Italian interface and the Appearance row, for review; (3) the Italian Learn topics, for review, through correttore\-italiano as the interface was — and with them Italian joins `SHIPPED`, so the Appearance row and the device fallback offer it. Study, journal and institution names stay as published.

## 3\. The Financial Model

The section most load\-bearing for everything else — a reframe arrived at over the course of this design conversation, correcting an earlier draft plan that would have contradicted §2 directly.

### 3\.1 Savings is the one real, foundational pool (Decided; unset state specified 2026\-09\-02)

Savings is not an Account (§7 — Credit, Loan), and not derived from Income — it is its own single figure, its own top\-level concept, present in exactly one place in the data model per Profile (§8). It is what the app supposes exists, whether or not the person has any income at all.

Savings **can go negative** — nothing in §2.3's non\-blocking philosophy would justify preventing an over\-committed Goal/Category/Recurring\-Expense transfer from taking it there. A negative Savings figure is shown plainly, with the same no\-valence\-color treatment as everywhere else (§2.1) — it's information, not a locked gate.

**`Savings.amount` is nullable, and unset is a real third state distinct from zero (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — closes a gap §11.7 depends on and §12 never declared).** On a fresh install it is `null`, because §11.1's flow never asks for it and §11.7 makes discovery the point.

- **`null` suppresses the checks that need a Savings figure to compare against** — §3.5's no\-income threshold, §5.1's Age of Money and its prospective toggle, and §6.4's sustainability view **when Income is off**. §6.4's Income\-on branch reads `incomeReceivedThisMonth` and touches Savings not at all (§6.4, rebased 2026\-09\-02), so it runs normally with Savings unset — an earlier version of this bullet suppressed it unconditionally, which would have silently disabled a check that has everything it needs. A brand\-new person who adds a €60 phone plan before entering any Savings should not be met with a threshold computed from a figure they were never asked for; this is the same instinct as §3.3's "hidden entirely, not greyed/disabled."
- **A genuine €0 is a real answer and behaves like one.** Someone who honestly logs €0 has told the app something true, so §11.7's message clears and the ≤ 0 rules (§3.5, §5.1, §6.4) apply exactly as specified. Treating a deliberate zero as "not set yet" would tell a person in genuine distress that their honest answer doesn't count — the precise failure §2.4's register exists to prevent.
- **Any action that would move Savings ends the `null` state rather than being blocked by it (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** A Recurring Expense sweep, a Category or Goal quick\-add, a general "\+" Money\-out — §11.7 explicitly invites all of these on day one, before Savings has been entered. Such an action treats the starting figure as €0 and applies its own movement normally, so a €60 sweep against unset Savings yields −€60, which §3.1 already permits and displays plainly. Suppressing the movement instead would contradict §3.11's "committed the moment the Recurring Expense exists," and leaving the field `null` while writing the Transaction would break §5.1's lot invariant permanently. **No opening lot is written by a zero (2026\-09\-02):** §5.1's lots are dated parcels of money, and a €0 starting figure has nothing to date — the first lot is opened by the first *positive* Savings movement, whenever that arrives. Until then Age of Money is floored at 0 by the Savings ≤ 0 rule, which is the correct reading either way.
- **While `null`, the figure position renders an em dash, never "€0.00"** — a zero must stay distinguishable from an absent answer, which is the whole reason the third state exists. Reports → Buffer, which is always present (§4.6), renders a single plain line saying the figure appears once Savings has a value; trend points earlier than the opening lot are omitted rather than drawn as zero. *(Built in the engine 2026\-09\-25: the screen had trimmed every leading zero instead, which also dropped a real zero after the opening lot.)*

### 3\.2 Everything else is just a labeled portion of Savings (Decided)

- **Goals** are savings with an objective (a name, a target amount, a timeline).
- **Categories** are temporary savings, earmarked for a recurring purpose (discretionary spending, checked plan\-vs\-actual each month).
- **Recurring Expenses** rely solely on Savings — never blended with Accounts (§7), and never checked against "everything the person owns."

This means every real money movement against a Goal, Category, or Recurring Expense is a **transfer against Savings** — not a soft, notional tally living only inside that item. Logging €150 toward a goal genuinely reduces Savings by €150, the same way logging a €12 coffee against Dining Out does. One mechanism, not a different rule to remember per item type.

**Money held in a Goal is out of the pool every Savings\-facing figure reads — deliberately (Decided, 2026\-09\-02; rationale sharpened 2026\-09\-02 on review).** The transfer principle above subtracts a goal contribution from `Savings.amount`, and nothing adds it back anywhere. That is correct, not an oversight, and it rests on three separate arguments rather than one:

- **The money already has a purpose, so it isn't available *whenever*.** An Emergency Fund exists to be spent in unpredictable quantities at a moment's notice; a Summer Holiday goal exists to be spent all at once across a planned stretch. Neither is money that is free for general use — which is exactly what a Savings figure is claiming to describe.
- **Counting it as spent is what keeps it actually available.** This is the point that makes the rule more than conservatism: an emergency fund only works if it is genuinely untouched when the emergency arrives, and the reliable way to leave it untouched is for it never to appear as spendable room in the first place. A figure that added it back would invite the person to spend against it slowly and find it gone at the moment it was needed — the earmarking research already warns that a label people can see through is a label they will spend through (§2.6, Soman & Cheema).
- **Anything else double\-counts.** The same euro would appear once in Savings and once in the Goal, and every threshold and report built on Savings would read a larger number than the person actually has. Giving an accurate, smaller figure is worth more than giving a flattering one.

**The consequence, stated plainly rather than discovered later:** §5.1's Age of Money, §5.3's Savings vs. Owed, §3.5's 1/12\-of\-Savings base and §6.4's Income\-off base all read unallocated `Savings.amount` only. Funding a Goal therefore *lowers* Buffer and lowers Savings vs. Owed. That is the honest reading: the unallocated cushion genuinely did shrink, because that money is now spoken for.

**This does not conflict with §3.7's reason for keeping the name Savings** ("incentivize increasing Age of Money/Buffer"). Buffer measures the unallocated cushion, and moving money into a Goal genuinely reduces the unallocated cushion. Both figures stay legible as the same thing they always were; neither is quietly measuring something else.

**Categories work the same way for a different reason.** A Category's `spent` records money that genuinely left in the real world — not earmarked, gone. It is out of the pool because it is out of the person's hands, which is also why §3.9 exempts the monthly reset from crediting anything back.

**A cash withdrawal is an expense, and the "out of the person's hands" test is not what settles it (Decided, 2026\-09\-05).** Cash drawn from an account is the one case where that phrase reads the wrong way — the money is very much *in* the person's hands, and yet it has still left Savings. The test that actually applies is the one this section opens with: Savings is the pool, and a withdrawal reduces it. There is no second pool for the cash to arrive in, because §7.1 removed Cash as an account type outright as "structurally redundant" rather than merging it into anything.

So a withdrawal is recorded as spending, and that is the accurate reading rather than a conservative one. **Someone who wants to track the cash separately already has the mechanism, and it is not a new one:** §8.2 states that a second pool watched separately is a Goal with no completion condition, and §6.3 builds it — a no\-target Goal, contributed to and drawn down like any other, with no "achieved" state to reach. Which of the two readings applies is the person's choice, made by whether they create that Goal; it is not something the app should infer from a transaction's shape, and nothing in the model needs to distinguish a cash withdrawal from any other outflow.

**Why this is stated here rather than left implicit:** it only becomes ambiguous when something other than the person is classifying an outflow, which is why it surfaced during §13.1's category\-automation analysis. But it is a fact about the model, true whether or not any automation is ever built, so it belongs with the rule it qualifies.

**This transfer principle applies identically to direct edits, not just quick\-add (Decided, 2026\-07\-25 — closes a gap surfaced in a fresh\-context review).** Goal.saved and Category.spent stay directly editable via their own full editor screens (§3.6) — but an edit made there is still a real movement of a labeled portion of Savings, not a separate action exempt from §3.2's rule. See §3.6 for the mechanics.

### 3\.3 Income is optional, and deliberately doesn't carry emotional weight (Decided; cadence\-change and marker rules added 2026\-09\-02)

- A Settings toggle, **off by default** for a fresh install.
- When off, the Income field is **hidden entirely** from Budget → Envelopes (not greyed/disabled) — its absence shouldn't read as "something is missing."
- When toggled off, the stored value is **cleared, not remembered**. Turning income back on later starts blank. This is a deliberate non\-judgmental choice: the app should never quietly resurface a smaller number from a harder period (e.g., a lower\-paying job taken out of necessity) as a ghost value once things improve.
- **Income has a cadence, not a flat monthly figure (Decided, 2026\-07\-19 — corrects the original assumption).** Checked against actual practice: in the U.S., biweekly pay is most common (43% of employers, BLS), followed by weekly (27%) and semimonthly (\~20%) — monthly pay covers only a small minority. Europe is closer to the opposite, with monthly the norm and some countries permitting nothing else. A flat "monthly income" field would be a poor, inaccurate fit for most U.S. earners. Income is therefore **amount per paycheck \+ cadence** (`weekly | biweekly | semimonthly | monthly`), mirroring Recurring Expenses' own `amount + cadence` shape (§12) rather than inventing a second pattern — though as of §3.11's redesign, Recurring Expenses have moved to a real\-count/apportioned sweep rather than a smoothed monthly\-equivalent, so this is a shared shape, not a shared calculation.
- **`monthlyEquivalent()` — a rough comparison rate, scoped narrowly (Decided, revised 2026\-07\-23).** weekly ×4.333, biweekly ×2.167, semimonthly ×2, monthly ×1. This conversion exists only for calculators that need a ballpark monthly figure with no Savings math riding on it — currently just the Overtime & Self\-Worth calculator's Salaried path (§10.2). **It must never be used anywhere that reads against Savings or determines what's currently available to spend** — that includes the recurring\-expense threshold (§3.5) and goal feasibility (§6.2), both of which read `incomeReceivedThisMonth` instead, for the same reason §3.11 insists Recurring Expenses be counted for real rather than smoothed: a flat multiplier would let an occasional 3\-paycheck month, or the ordinary lag before a payday lands, misrepresent what's genuinely available right now.
- **`incomeReceivedThisMonth` — the real, landed figure Savings\-facing calculations use instead (Decided, 2026\-07\-23; derived rather than stored, 2026\-09\-02).** It is **not a stored counter**\: it is the sum of `isIncome` Transactions whose `date` falls in the current calendar month **and on or after `incomeEnabledDate`**, recomputed on read. Never counted in advance, never estimated. This mirrors the logic settled for Recurring Expenses (§3.11): expenses are spoken\-for the moment they're committed, because that money was never available for anything else; income gets the mirror\-image treatment — not counted until it has actually landed, because until then it isn't available either, no matter how confidently the date is known.
  - **Deriving it rather than storing it is what makes §3.9's two triggers genuinely order\-independent (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** A stored counter has to be zeroed by the month\-boundary trigger and incremented by the auto\-deposit catch\-up, and those two orders give different answers on the same day: an app reopened on 10 April with a 9 April deposit reads €1,200 if the reset runs first and €0 if the catch\-up does — the difference between §3.5's threshold flagging and clearing, and between §6.2 offering a goal and refusing one. Derived, there is no order to get wrong, and §3.9's claim is true by construction rather than by convention.
  - **It also removes three special cases that existed only to maintain the counter.** Its monthly "reset" is simply the calendar\-month filter (no reset logic, nothing for §3.9's catch\-up to replay). Its clearing on Income toggle\-off is simply `incomeEnabledDate` being cleared and re\-set, so re\-enabling Income mid\-month correctly starts from 0 rather than resuming — the behaviour previously specified, now falling out of the definition. And §7.3's pair\-deletion needs no decrement rule: removing the rows removes them from the sum. `incomeReceivedThisMonth` and `lastIncomeResetMonth` are accordingly dropped from §12's stored fields.
  - **What counts:** (a) every auto\-deposit Transaction — unambiguously income by construction; and (b) any manual entry logged via the dedicated **Income quick\-add button** (§3.6) — a separate button from general quick\-add, not a checkbox on a shared form, since a person who declined auto\-deposit has already opted out of the automated path once and shouldn't have income\-tagging reintroduced as an easy\-to\-miss field on the general path instead.
  - **Attribution is by `date`, not `loggedAt` and not processing time.** §3.9's catch\-up writes missed auto\-deposits with their real historical dates, so an app reopened on 10 April after being closed since 5 January replays deposits dated 19 January through 30 March. Counting those into April would read €7,200 of "income received this month" before a single April euro had landed — precisely the error the 2026\-07\-23 reversal eliminated. Attributing by `date` also handles a backdated manual entry correctly.
  - **Money already landed is unaffected by any of this.** Whatever arrived is, by then, ordinary untagged Savings — indistinguishable from any other euro in the pool, the same way a paycheck doesn't retroactively vanish because someone later quits the job that paid it. What the figure tracks is a live projection, not a balance.
- Someone with income off is not shown a hole where "income" should be — the entire point (audit §3.7, this conversation's founding reframe) is that managing an existing Savings pool well is itself the valuable asset, independent of having a paycheck. This is also why §5.4's Income vs Expenses report is hidden outright when Income is off rather than drawn with an empty series: money arriving without a paycheck — a birthday gift, a repayment from a friend — is Savings, and calling it "income" would misdescribe it.

**Getting the declared income into Savings — a choice, not an assumption (Decided, 2026\-07\-19):**

Automatically depositing the figure would remove real friction (re\-entering the same amount every payday); never automating it would leave a real, missable manual step. Rather than pick one, the person chooses:

- When Income is toggled **on**, a second toggle appears: **"Auto\-deposit income every payday?"** — off by default, consistent with §2.3's pattern of nothing being switched on without a conscious choice. **Turning it back on after an earlier on\-period starts from today (Decided, 2026\-09\-25, with the owner):** the stale `lastIncomeDepositDate` would otherwise replay every payday the switch was off as real, dated income — the invented money this section exists to prevent. A first switch\-on (no marker yet) is unchanged and replays from the chosen payday, below.
  - **On:** the declared per\-paycheck amount deposits into Savings automatically **on the real mechanics of the cadence chosen above** — see the anchor\-date bullet below — logged as a Transaction (`key: 'savings'`, payee "Income", `isIncome: true`) through the exact same mechanism quick\-add uses (§3.6). This is the corrected version of an earlier draft that assumed a fixed monthly deposit regardless of how the person is actually paid.
  - **Off (the manual path):** the **Income quick\-add button** (§3.6; **since 2026\-10\-09 the *Income* direction of the Savings sentence, which pre\-fills the same way**) pre\-fills its amount field with the declared per\-paycheck figure (still fully editable, still requiring the usual Confirm) rather than starting blank — removing the need to re\-type the number every payday without removing the deliberate step of confirming it actually arrived. Logging through this button always counts toward `incomeReceivedThisMonth` — pressing the dedicated Income button **is** the tagging decision.
- **Anchor date (Decided, 2026\-07\-20; semimonthly corrected 2026\-07\-23; scope broadened 2026\-07\-25; monthly clarified 2026\-07\-28):** rather than a generic system default (1st/15th) or a preference\-style picker, the app asks a plain fact question, once, right when it's needed — **"When's your next payday?"**, a single editable date field defaulting to today, in the same register as asking for a Loan's term or an Account's balance. **Each subsequent deposit follows the real mechanics of its own cadence, not one flat day\-count rule:**
