  - **Weekly and biweekly are genuine fixed intervals** — every 7 or 14 days, drifting across calendar dates over the year exactly as real biweekly pay does.
  - **Monthly is calendar\-day anchored, not a flat \+30 days** — it recurs on the same day of the month every cycle, snapping to the last day of shorter months (a payday anchored on the 30th lands on the 28th in February), reusing the identical mechanic §3.11 defines for Recurring Expenses' fixed\-date mode. Real monthly pay is essentially never a rolling 30\-day interval drifting across the year.
  - **Semimonthly asks the same plain fact question twice** — "When's your next payday?" followed by "And the second one?" — populating `incomePayDate` and `incomePayDate2` (§12) as two fixed calendar dates (e.g. the 15th and last day), each snapping at month\-end by the same rule, rather than deriving the second from the first by adding an interval. Real semimonthly pay doesn't reduce to a clean fixed interval, and guessing the second date would reintroduce exactly the drift semimonthly exists to avoid.
  - Unlike Recurring Expenses, Income's cadence carries no separate user\-facing date\-mode choice: which mechanic applies is implicit in the cadence itself, since a payday's fixed\-vs\-drifting nature isn't something a person would meaningfully choose the way a subscription's billing style might vary.
  - The payday date(s) live in **Settings → Income section**, editable at any time, and follow the same non\-remembering rule as `income` itself. **This question is asked whenever Income is turned on, regardless of the auto\-deposit choice** — originally scoped to auto\-deposit only, but the manual\-path "no income logged" check below needs the same anchor date just as much, to know when a paycheck was expected.
- **Changing `incomeCadence` while Income stays on re\-asks the anchor question (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** **Re\-asked means empty until answered (Decided, 2026\-09\-25, with the owner):** the payday field shows a *"Choose a date"* placeholder while no date is stored, and nothing auto\-deposits until one is chosen — the build had shown today's date in a field the engine held as unset. Semimonthly needs both dates; with only the first chosen it is not treated as monthly. The Income section is editable at any time, so a person can switch cadence after a job change while Income never goes off. When `incomeCadence` changes, the app asks the plain fact question again for the new cadence — twice for semimonthly, once otherwise — clears whichever date field the new cadence doesn't use (`incomePayDate2` when leaving semimonthly), and resets `lastIncomeDepositDate` to null so no cycle can be retroactively invented across the change. Without this, switching monthly → semimonthly leaves `incomePayDate2` null while both auto\-deposit and the check below read it, and switching the other way leaves a stale second date populated for a cadence §12 says should never have one.
- **"No income logged in the last X days" — a passive check for the manual path (Decided, 2026\-07\-25; mechanism updated 2026\-07\-28; X defined 2026\-09\-02).** The dedicated Income quick\-add button still requires the person to actively choose it over the general "\+" button — someone on the manual path can simply tap the wrong one, payday after payday, and `incomeReceivedThisMonth` would silently read low with nothing visibly wrong. This is the non\-punitive safety net for exactly that gap.
  - **Fires only when all three are true:** Income is enabled; auto\-deposit is **off** (this never appears for automated users — there's nothing for them to forget); and real elapsed time since the reference point below has passed the cadence interval implied by `incomeCadence`.
  - **Reference point:** the most recent isIncome\-tagged Transaction's date (`lastIncomeLoggedDate`, §12), or, if none has been logged since Income was last turned on, **`incomeEnabledDate` (§12, added 2026\-09\-02)** — the date Income was most recently switched on. That date needed a field of its own: `lastIncomeLoggedDate` clears on toggle\-off, so it is null at precisely the moment the fallback is needed. Both clear on toggle\-off, the same non\-resurfacing rule already applied to `income`/`incomePayDate` — without which, re\-enabling Income after months off would immediately surface a stale, alarming "no income logged in 60\+ days" reading before the person had any chance to log anything.
  - **Cadence interval:** weekly \= 7 days, biweekly \= 14 days, monthly \= the calendar\-day anchor's next occurrence. **Semimonthly** doesn't reduce to a flat day\-count any more than it does anywhere else in this spec — it checks against whichever of `incomePayDate`/`incomePayDate2` most recently passed.
  - **X is today minus the reference point** — the same value the fire condition is tested against, so the number shown and the number tested are one figure rather than two. It is the real, growing elapsed\-day count, not the fixed cadence number, so it keeps being true the longer it goes unaddressed. (Counting from the missed payday instead would show "7 days" where this shows "37," from identical data.)
  - **Display:** *"No income logged in the last X days."* Presented with the same non\-blocking `feasibility-note` styling as everywhere else (§2.3, §3.5) — a plain fact, not a warning color, not a nag, and per §2.3's ambient\-note rule it carries no corrective clause. It clears the moment any isIncome Transaction lands, correct amount or not.
  - **Placement:** Budget → Envelopes, in the Income section (§4.2) — the section the check is about. (This bullet previously justified its placement by reference to the Income checkbox, a control the 2026\-07\-28 revision replaced with the two buttons on the Savings card.)
- **Toggling Income off** resets `income` to 0 and turns auto\-deposit off — there's no figure left to deposit. It also clears `incomePayDate`, `incomePayDate2`, `lastIncomeLoggedDate`, `incomeEnabledDate`, and **`lastIncomeDepositDate` (added 2026\-09\-02 — an omission, not a decision).** That last one matters more than its size suggests: it is the marker §3.9's auto\-deposit catch\-up compares against now, and catch\-up replays every elapsed cycle with real historical dates. Left uncleared, a person who turns Income off during a hard stretch and back on eight months later would have roughly seventeen biweekly paychecks they never received deposited into Savings as real, dated Transactions — money the app invented, propagating straight into §5.1's Age of Money, §5.3's Savings vs. Owed, §3.5's threshold and §6.2's feasibility read, all of which are ledger\-derived. `incomeReceivedThisMonth` needs no clearing rule of its own now that it is derived: clearing `incomeEnabledDate` is what zeroes it. **When auto\-deposit is next switched on, `lastIncomeDepositDate` starts null and the first automatic deposit is the `incomePayDate` the person just supplied** — never a backfill from an earlier era. Toggling off never reaches backward: money already deposited is already real Savings and is never clawed back. The toggle only ever controlled *future* deposits.

### 3\.4 \[Removed, 2026\-07\-23\]

Investment tracking has been removed from the app entirely (§13.4) — no Settings figure, no Savings vs. Owed contribution, nothing. This section number is intentionally left retired rather than renumbering §3.5–§3.11 (and every cross\-reference to them elsewhere in this document) down by one — the same low\-risk\-over\-tidiness call already made once in this document's own history when "Not yet assigned" was retired without reclaiming its old name (§3.7).

### 3\.5 Recurring\-expense threshold (Decided, 2026\-07\-21; income side reversed 2026\-07\-23; expense side clarified 2026\-09\-02)

- **No income:** flag once recurring expenses exceed **1/12 of Savings** — at that pace, they alone could exhaust the whole balance within about a year. **At Savings ≤ 0, this fires unconditionally (Decided, scoped 2026\-07\-23)** — the threshold itself becomes €0 or negative, and any positive recurring\-expense total exceeds it. No special case needed: this is the correct answer, not a degenerate one — any ongoing commitment is unsustainable with no income and no cushion left, so the flag should fire every time, not be suppressed because the math looks unusual at the boundary. **At Savings `null` (never set, §3.1) the check does not run at all** — there is no figure to take a twelfth of.
- **Income on:** flag once recurring expenses exceed **1/3 of income received so far this month** (`incomeReceivedThisMonth`, §3.3). Savings is deliberately excluded from this check, for the same reason it's excluded from goal feasibility (§6.2): a comfortable Savings balance would otherwise silently mask a genuinely unsustainable monthly rate.
- Presented via the same non\-blocking, non\-verdict `feasibility-note` styling used everywhere else in the app (§2.3) — as an *ambient* note, so per §2.3's 2026\-09\-02 scoping it states the fact and carries no save\-it\-as\-is clause.
- This is a genuinely different signal from goal feasibility (§6.2), not a duplicate of it — one is about the weight of Recurring Expenses on their own, the other is about a specific Goal's fit against what's left over. **Both can surface at once**, and should: they answer different questions, and showing one shouldn't suppress the other.
- **The income side is a real, landed figure (Decided, reversed 2026\-07\-23 — this reverses, rather than amends, the 2026\-07\-21 resolution that had kept income smoothed while expenses became real).** `incomeReceivedThisMonth` is the actual amount that has landed in Savings this calendar month, not a projected or averaged one. The earlier reasoning didn't survive contact with the app's own stated principle that money isn't available until it actually exists — a Savings\-availability check has no business crediting a paycheck that hasn't landed yet, any more than it would credit an expense that hasn't come due.
- **The expense side is the month's *scheduled* occurrence total, not the amount swept so far (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Both figures use §3.11's own count/apportionment math for the current calendar month, evaluated whether or not the 1st\-of\-month sweep has already fired, and never counting an occurrence earlier than the expense's `firstDueDate`. This is what §3.11's governing rule forces: an expense is committed "the moment the Recurring Expense exists," and the sweep trigger is explicitly "administrative front\-loading, not a claim that every bill is literally due that day." Reading swept\-to\-date instead would produce a real hole — someone adding €830/month of expenses on the 18th, all with due dates already passed, sweeps nothing that month (§3.11's creation\-timing rules) and would see no note at all at exactly the moment they took the commitment on. It also settles deletion cleanly: a deleted expense has no remaining scheduled occurrences and drops out of the figure immediately, with no separate rule needed.
- **At a zero or absent base the flag still fires, but the sentence states the figure and stops (Decided, 2026\-09\-03).** §6.4 met this exact problem — a base of €0 is the normal state at the start of every Income\-on month — and answered it by suppressing the arithmetic in favour of a plain\-fact line. §3.5 never got the same treatment, and the sentence it produced instead was *"Recurring expenses so far this month (€1,364) are more than a third of the €0 you've received so far"*\: a ratio taken against nothing, which reads as alarm rather than as the fact it is. The rule, matching §6.4's: **name the scheduled figure, name the state of the base, take no ratio.** *"Recurring expenses scheduled this month come to €1,364. No income has landed yet this month"* with Income on; *"…with no Savings cushion to draw them from"* with Income off and Savings ≤ 0. **The trigger is unaffected** — it fires exactly as the two bullets above specify; only the copy changes, and only where the base is ≤ 0.
- **A predictable consequence, not a bug (Decided, 2026\-07\-23):** for an Income\-on user, this threshold will often read as flagged for a stretch at the start of most months — the full month's recurring commitment is counted from the 1st while paychecks arrive gradually across the month — and clear as income lands. This is the same kind of honest lumpiness §3.11 already documents for a biweekly bill debiting 2 or 3 times some months; it's accurate, cyclical behavior, not a signal that needs smoothing away.

### 3\.6 Quick\-add: the low\-friction path, never the only path (Decided; direction, sign and deletion specified 2026\-09\-02; one entry sentence for every editable element, 2026\-10\-09)

Available on Savings, Goals, Categories, and Accounts. Captures **amount, an optional payee/note, and a date** (defaulting to today). ~~Confirming requires an explicit **Confirm/Cancel** pair — deliberately, so the app never needs an "undo" affordance.~~ **Superseded 2026\-10\-09 (below): one *Done*, and dismissing writes nothing — still never an undo.**

**One entry for every editable element (Decided, 2026\-10\-09, with the owner — prototype `design/entry-prototype.html`, scored by `tools/entry_kinds_score.py` and `tools/selector_score.py`; built in `engine/Entry.kt` and `ui/sheets/EntrySheet.kt`).** Every \+ opens one sheet, titled **Log**, holding one sentence whose underlined words are its own controls. A tapped word opens its options above the sentence, so the sentence stays at the sheet's foot:

- **Savings** – *Add* / *Income* € \[amount\] *today*, from \[who or where\]
- **Goal** – *Add* € \[amount\] to *\[goal\]* *today*, for \[what\] · *Spent* € \[amount\] from *\[goal\]* *today*, at \[where\]
- **Category** – Spent € \[amount\] on *\[category\]* *today*, at \[where\]
- **Account** – *Charged* € \[amount\] on *\[card\]* *today*, at \[where\] · *Paid off* € \[amount\] on *\[account\]* *today*, *also from Savings* / *not from Savings* (a Loan: *Paid off* / *Increased*)

The rules it carries:

- **The area word comes first and is itself a control** (owner: "a clickable word in front of the sentence, not a horizontal switch"). Choosing another area opens its names at once, on the one used last; Savings has one target, so it has no target word. Measured against a four\-way switch row at 360dp: the sheet is 250–298 px against 306–354 px (mean 274 against 321), for one more tap per change of area, which only the floating \+ needs.
- **Where an entry starts.** Each row's \+ opens the sentence on that row's element. The **floating \+** (phone only), the desktop's **Add** (since 2026\-10\-22, below) and **Ctrl\+N** (desktop) open the last area, target and direction logged in this session, or the first Category before anything is logged. ~~On a desktop every row, Savings included, carries its own \+, and there is neither a floating \+ nor a single Add button — the owner: the desktop "doesn't need a single button to do everything," and a corner Add "seems to reduce efficiency rather than improve it."~~ **Superseded 2026\-10\-22 (owner, after the Add mockups and a live test): the desktop keeps every row's \+ and adds one labelled *Add*, fixed at the top right of the content — the right edge of the right\-most column, not the window's — on Budget and Activity, where the phone shows its floating \+.** It opens the last area and target, as Ctrl\+N does, and stays put while the page scrolls. Measured over seven logging tasks at four window and text sizes (`design/mockups-2026-10-17/add-live.html`), rows and Add together beat rows alone and Add alone on time (26.4 s against 28.3 and 38.8), clicks (8 against 9 and 19), pointer travel and pointing difficulty, need no scroll\-to\-find for a repeat entry, and keep a labelled entry point on every page; the cost is one more control on screen.
- **Done, not Confirm/Cancel** (owner, 2026\-10\-09): one *Done* at the title's height, at its end, not bold, dimmed until the readout is complete. Dismissing the sheet — the scrim, Back, a swipe, Esc — writes nothing. The cursor starts in the amount, and Enter writes once the readout is complete; otherwise it does nothing.
- **The readout and the captions.** Under the sentence a readout says what will move (*"Holidays \+€50 · Savings −€50"*), and four captions, approved as written: Income *"Counts toward this month's income received."*; a note naming a recurring expense *"\[name\] is a recurring expense and is taken on its own date. Log it here only if this is an extra payment."*; a Goal's *Spent* above what it holds *"\[goal\] holds €\[saved\], so this can spend up to that."*; and a *Paid off* states whether Savings moves, by the account's linkage (§7.2 — *"Savings stays: \[expense\] already takes this from Savings on its date."* or, switched on anyway, *"…so this would count it twice."*).
- **Savings has no Money out** (owner: *"all expenses and movements should be accounted for, tracked within Goals, Categories, etc. They can't just disappear"*). Every expense is spent on a Category, spent from a Goal, or charged to or paid on an Account. A cash purchase is a Category; cash drawn from an ATM can be kept in a Goal with no deadline (§6.3), if the person wants, and spent from it later. Savings takes *Add* and, while Income is on, *Income* (§3.3); with Income off it has one direction and so no control.
- **A Goal can be spent from** (new): `Goal.saved` goes down by at most what it holds, Savings does not move — the money left Savings when it went into the Goal — and it is logged under its own key, `spend:<goalId>` (table below). Categories remain one\-direction.
- **The note's word** is *from* on Savings, *for* on a Goal's *Add*, *at* on spending and charges, and absent on a *Paid off*, where the log already reads *"Payment to \[account\]"*. Its placeholder never repeats the word (*for* \[what\], not *for* \[what for\] — found on the 2026\-10\-10 device walk).
- **Back closes the sheet and writes nothing (2026\-10\-10 device walk).** With the keyboard up, the first Back hides it and the second closes the sheet. Back had never closed any sheet: the host only popped pushes, and a focused text field keeps Back to itself, so the entry's fields hand it to the sheet. Every sheet now closes on Back except §3.11's Reconcile, which must be answered.
- **The floating \+ steps aside while §11.3's goal prompt is up** — it had been drawn over the prompt's *"Let's set one"* (2026\-10\-10 device walk). **So does the desktop's Add (2026\-10\-23):** it is drawn over the page, and the prompt is a sheet inside the page.
- **No typed capture line.** A line on every screen was built and scored against the sentence alone (five ways to find a name in it, C1–C5, over fourteen lines of the seed's real names): the sentence won every task but one — Income logged away from Budget on a desktop, by 0.08 s — needs no grammar learned, and keeps 56dp on every screen. An offline language model was assessed and not needed: the names are a closed, known list, and mapping "fun" to *Entertainment* is the guess §4.3 forbids.

**Direction is an explicit two\-option control where both directions are real, and fixed by the target where only one is (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — the sign convention was previously unstated, and the document required opposite readings in different places).** No quick\-add field ever accepts a typed minus sign:

- **Goals and Categories: one direction only.** Quick\-add moves money *into* the label — `Goal.saved` up, `Category.spent` up, Savings down by the same amount. Reductions and corrections go through the item's own full editor screen (below), which is where §3.6's worked correction examples already put them.
- ~~**Savings: the general "\+" button's form carries a Money out / Money in control**, defaulting to **Money out**, since the unlabeled event a person most often needs to record is a purchase that belongs to no Category. Without this, a €40 cash purchase would have no path except the full editor's typed\-UPDATE prompt — a confirmation this section explicitly reserves "for corrections, not additions."~~ **Superseded 2026\-10\-09: Savings has no Money out; the €40 cash purchase is spent on a Category (above).** Goals gained a second direction the same day, *Spent* (above).
- **Accounts: the same two\-option control, labelled in the account's own terms** — Charge / Payment on a Credit account, Payment / Increase on a Loan (**worded *Charged* / *Paid off* and *Paid off* / *Increased* since 2026\-10\-09**). §7.2 requires both directions (logging a card purchase raises the balance; recording an extra loan payment lowers it), and a single add\-only field would silently record an extra payment as new debt.

**`Transaction.amount` is the signed delta applied to the named target's own tracked quantity (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** This is the convention every ledger\-reading feature depends on, and it must be stated once rather than inferred per section:

| `key` | `amount` is the delta to | Savings moves by |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `savings` | `Savings.amount` | `+amount` |
| `goal:<id>` | `Goal.saved` | `−amount` |
| `cat:<id>` | `Category.spent` | `−amount` |
| `rec:<id>` | the amount swept for that expense (positive \= swept out) | `−amount` |
| `acct:<id>` | `Account.balance` | `0` — Accounts are not part of the Savings pool (§7.1) |
| `spend:<goalId>` | `Goal.saved` (negative: spent from the Goal; added 2026\-10\-09) | `0` — it left Savings when it went into the Goal |

So a €20 Category quick\-add stores `{key: 'cat:x', amount: +20}` and moves Savings −€20; a Recurring Expense sweep of €100 stores `+100` and moves Savings −€100, while its deletion reversal stores `−100` and moves Savings \+€100. §5.1's FIFO engine, §5.2's shares, §5.3's history and §7.3's pair\-matching all execute against this table rather than against prose. Under the opposite reading — storing the Savings effect rather than the target delta — every Goal and Category row inverts and Age of Money rises when money is spent.

**Superseded 2026\-10\-09 by the entry sentence above:** the Savings card carries no buttons (a tap opens its full editor), and Income is a direction of the Savings sentence rather than a separate "↓" button — choosing it is still the tagging decision, and it still exists only while Income is on. **Savings gets two separate quick\-add buttons, not a shared form with a checkbox (Decided, 2026\-07\-28 — replaces the 2026\-07\-23 checkbox design).** A general **"\+"** button, and, visible only when Income is enabled (§3.3), a dedicated **"↓"** Income button — physically separate entry points on the Savings card rather than one form with an easy\-to\-miss toggle field. Logging through the Income button always tags the resulting Transaction (`isIncome: true`, §12), always records Money in, and counts toward `incomeReceivedThisMonth` (§3.3); the general "\+" button never tags — no checkbox needed either way, since which button the person tapped **is** the tagging decision. When Income is off, the Income button simply isn't there, consistent with §3.3's "hidden entirely, not greyed/disabled" treatment. Both buttons confirm through the same lightweight Confirm/Cancel pair; the heavier confirmation below is reserved for corrections, not additions. **The Savings card carries the identical affordance set wherever it appears** — Budget → Envelopes and Activity alike (§3.7, §4.4): both buttons, the §11.7 message while `Savings.amount` is null, and the entry point into the full editor. §3.7's "the same number read in two places" is only true if the affordances match.

**Savings full editor screen (Decided, 2026\-07\-28) — given the same standing as Goal.saved and Category.spent's own editor screens, reflecting that Savings is the single most load\-bearing figure in the app.** Reached from the Savings card on either screen, it edits `Savings.amount` directly rather than logging an additive entry. Saving requires typing the word **UPDATE** into an unskippable prompt before the edit takes effect — a deliberately heavier confirmation than the Confirm/Cancel pair used everywhere else, chosen specifically because a list of toggles or a second button is easier to miss\-tap than it is to type a word by accident. **It logs a Transaction carrying the signed delta, never the new absolute value (Decided, 2026\-09\-02)** — raising Savings from €150 to €1,000 writes `{key: 'savings', amount: +850}`, dated to the moment of the edit. An absolute\-value row would be a second, competing statement of truth in a log §12 defines as "history only," and would break §5.1's lot arithmetic permanently. Edits here never tag `isIncome` — the two quick\-add buttons already cover every path into `incomeReceivedThisMonth`; the full editor exists for corrections, not for logging new income by another route.

Critically: **quick\-add is additive, never a replacement for direct editing.** Every field it touches (**Savings.amount**, Goal.saved, Category.spent, Account.balance) remains a plain, directly\-editable number via its own full editor screen at all times. This corrects an earlier draft plan (surfaced and withdrawn mid\-conversation) that would have made these fields transaction\-derived and read\-only once a Transaction model existed — that would have contradicted §2.3/§3.2's own reasoning directly: the person may need to spend more than planned, or correct a misassigned expense, and a static, non\-editable field would make the app unfit for how daily life actually behaves.

**Direct edits to Goal.saved or Category.spent move Savings by the inverse of the delta, and log a Transaction — the editor screen is a second entry point to the same mechanism quick\-add uses, not a separate one (Decided, 2026\-07\-25).** Concretely:

- Quick\-add €50 to a Goal ("beach trip") → Goal.saved \+€50, Savings −€50, logged as a Transaction.
- Quick\-add €20 to a Category ("groceries") → Category.spent \+€20, Savings −€20, logged as a Transaction.
- Directly editing Category.spent to *subtract* €25 (correcting a purchase mistakenly logged against "groceries" instead of "hobby supplies") → Category.spent −€25, **Savings \+€25**, logged as a Transaction — releasing that €25 back to general Savings, exactly as the earmarking research (§2.6, Soman & Cheema) already argues these labels must stay freely editable rather than guilt\-gated.
- Quick\-adding that same €25 to the correct Category ("hobby supplies") → Category.spent \+€25, Savings −€25, logged as a Transaction.

The net effect of a correction like the one above is Savings\-neutral across the two actions, exactly as it should be — the money never actually moved in the real world, only the label did. **This is why the rule has to be symmetric (any delta, either direction, on Goal.saved or Category.spent, moves Savings by the exact inverse):** anything short of that would let the labeled total drift away from what's actually been pulled from Savings, recreating the two\-sources\-of\-truth failure §3.7 and §7.3 already fight elsewhere. **Scope note:** this rule governs *person\-initiated* edits only — it does not apply to Category.spent's automated monthly reset to 0 (§3.9), which is a period\-counter clear, not a correction, and is explicitly exempted there for exactly this reason. Each such Transaction carries the delta as its `amount` (signed, per the table above), is dated to the moment of the edit (not backdated), and defaults its payee/note to something like "Correction" unless the person supplies their own. The editor screen's Save action requires the same Confirm/Cancel discipline as quick\-add (above) for exactly the same reason: it's moving real money, not just relabeling a display value.

**Deleting a Goal or a Category (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — both were previously unspecified, though §7.3 already assumed both were possible).** The two are deliberately different, because the money behind them is:

- **Deleting a Goal with `saved` \> 0 shows an unskippable prompt — *"Add €\[saved\] back to Savings?"*** — and, on accept, credits Savings and logs a Transaction (`key: goal:<id>`, `amount: −saved`). That money was *earmarked*, not spent: it never left the person's hands, and §2.6's earmarking finding is explicit that a label must stay freely releasable rather than guilt\-gated. This is the same prompt shape §3.11 already uses for deleting a Recurring Expense with an un\-elapsed swept amount. Declining leaves Savings untouched. **The prompt's shape, and a confirmation step (Decided, 2026\-09\-15 — chosen from three mockups, `design/fix-options.html`):** the accepting action *Delete and add back* on its own line, then *Cancel* / *Delete only* — the prototype's row of three breaks its third label into four lines at 360dp. Either delete then leads to a second step in the same sheet that states exactly what is about to happen — *"€\[saved\] goes back to Savings and \[name\] is deleted. Its past history stays in the log."* or *"\[name\] is deleted. Savings stays as it is."* — with *Go back* / *Delete*. A confirmation rather than an undo, by the person's choice: the consequence is read before it happens, not after. With nothing to add back there is one question and one *Cancel* / *Delete* row, which is already the confirmation. The recurring\-expense prompt (§3.11) has the same shape; both are one composable (`ui/components/DeleteSheets.kt`).
- **The Trash (Decided 2026\-10\-19/20 with the owner, after the mockups; built 2026\-10\-20).** Every delete — a Goal, a Category, a Recurring Expense, an Account (with the payment it was created with, as one), a Profile, §7.3's cancelled pair — still happens at once and still asks the money question above when it does, but what it removed is **kept in the Trash for 30 days** (Settings › Trash), with **Restore** and **Delete forever**. **Restore undoes exactly what the delete did, money included, as new logged rows** (*"Restored from the Trash"*): a Goal whose saved money went back to Savings takes it back out; a reversed sweep is taken again; a closed Account's balance is logged back on. History is never rewritten — the delete's rows and the restore's both stay in the ledger. **While in the Trash an item is inert:** it is out of the Profile, so a payment sweeps nothing and reminds of nothing and nothing counts towards any figure; a restored payment resumes from that month, with no catch\-up for the months it spent there. A Profile comes back whole, as one more Profile, and is not opened. **At 20 days one notification** names what will go for good in ten; at 30 days it goes, by the daily background task or on the next open. The Trash is device\-only (`trash`, schema 8): a backup carries what exists, not what was deleted, and **restoring a backup empties the Trash**. The money question is answered at the delete, so Delete forever and the 30\-day expiry have nothing left to ask. §7.2's Loan\-to\-Credit retype removes its payment without the Trash: it is a save, not a delete.
- **Deleting a Category returns nothing and writes no Transaction.** Its `spent` records money that genuinely left in the real world, purchase by purchase. Crediting it back on delete would be the identical phantom\-refund bug §3.9 was amended to prevent for the monthly reset — a €340 category deleted mid\-month would hand back €340 as if the month's shopping had been undone.

In both cases the item's existing Transactions stay in the ledger, keeping their `targetLabel` snapshot (§7.3), so the history doesn't orphan.

**Account.balance is the one exception to the Savings\-movement rule, and stays one (§7.1 unchanged):** Accounts are liabilities, not labeled portions of Savings, so editing a Credit or Loan balance directly never touches Savings — the same distinction that already makes them subtract in Savings vs. Owed (§5.3) rather than participate in the Savings pool at all. **This exempts Account edits from moving Savings, not from being logged (Decided, 2026\-07\-28):** quick\-add, direct edits, **and Loan auto\-update decrements (§7.2, added 2026\-09\-02)** all write a Transaction (`key: acct:<id>`) — pure record\-keeping with no Savings\-side effect, needed so Savings vs. Owed's own history (§5.3) can be derived from the ledger the same way every other Report already is.

**Recurring Expenses are not on this list (Decided, 2026\-07\-21 — corrects the earlier draft).** They're automatic, swept against Savings on schedule rather than manually logged (§3.11) — there's no occurrence left for quick\-add to log. Quick\-add on Savings remains the general\-purpose fallback for anything that needs correcting, including a Recurring Expense's sweep. **Since 2026\-10\-09 that fallback is the Savings full editor (below), since the Savings sentence only adds; and a note naming a recurring expense in a Category's or Goal's sentence says it is taken on its own date.**

**The entered `date` affects the ledger, never the live field (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** The precedence rule this rests on, stated here rather than left to be inferred from §12's Transaction row: **the log is a record, not the source of truth for the field it touches; the field's own current value is.** So `Savings.amount`, `Goal.saved`, `Category.spent` and `Account.balance` all move at the moment of the action, whatever date was entered. The `date` field governs only the ledger and everything derived from it — §5.1's FIFO walk, §5.2's monthly shares, §5.3's history, §5.4's series, and `incomeReceivedThisMonth`'s month attribution (§3.3). A €120 grocery run logged on 2 March and backdated to 28 February therefore increments March's `Category.spent` while the Spending report files it under February; that divergence is intended, and is the cost of letting the person record when something really happened without retroactively rewriting a closed month's counters.

Every quick\-add and direct edit is logged as a **Transaction** (§12), for the full ledger view (§7.3). A narrow, scoped exception to the ledger's otherwise permanent, append\-only nature is described in §7.3.

### 3\.7 "Not yet assigned" is retired as a separate concept (Decided)

An earlier draft treated "Not yet assigned" as a computed cushion figure distinct from Savings. It is now simply **Savings**, shown identically wherever it appears (Budget → Envelopes, and Activity) — the same number read in two places, not two sources of truth. It kept the name Savings rather than being renamed again, specifically because the intent is to **incentivize increasing Age of Money/Buffer** (§5.1) — that framing only works if the figure driving it is legible as the same thing everywhere.

### 3\.8 Leftover money (Decided)

No separate rollover bucket. Unspent, unassigned money simply stays in Savings — exactly what a real bank account already does. Nothing computes or displays a "leftover from last month" figure; it's just part of the same ongoing balance.

### 3\.9 The monthly cycle (Decided, 2026\-07\-19; mechanics specified 2026\-07\-28 and 2026\-09\-02 — ~~implementation still pending~~ built 2026\-09\-14, §13.3)

**One shared definition, not a per\-feature invention:** "this month" means the current calendar month, everywhere it's used — Categories, the Spending report, all of it. Categories' own reset uses the same boundary rather than a separate concept.

- **Categories:** `spent` resets to 0 at the start of a new calendar month; `planned` carries forward automatically rather than requiring re\-entry. Re\-asking for the same number every month would be exactly the kind of decision\-fatigue the audit's automation findings argue against (§2.6, audit §3.2/§4.1) — the plan stays freely editable at any time (§3.6), it just doesn't *demand* a fresh answer each cycle. **This reset is explicitly exempt from §3.6's direct\-edit\-to\-Savings rule (Decided, 2026\-07\-25 — closes a real bug that rule would otherwise have introduced).** Zeroing `spent` at the month boundary is a bookkeeping reset of a period counter, not a correction of a mistaken entry — the money already left Savings for real, transaction by transaction, over the month it was actually spent. Reading the reset as a −€340 → €0 "edit" under §3.6's inverse rule would credit Savings €340 back as if the whole month's spending had been undone. The New Month engine's reset writes no Transaction and moves nothing in Savings; only a person\-initiated change on the editor screen does.
- **Income needs no reset at all (2026\-09\-02).** `incomeReceivedThisMonth` is derived rather than stored (§3.3) — it is a filter over the ledger by calendar month, so the month boundary changes its value without anything having to run.
- **Missed cycles are caught up retroactively, never silently skipped.** If the app goes unopened across one or more real cycles (a missed Category reset, elapsed Recurring\-Expense sweeps per §3.11, or elapsed Income/auto\-deposit intervals per §3.3), the next app\-open counts how many cycles actually elapsed and catches all of them up in sequence, with their real historical dates — not "today" — logged as Transactions. Silently skipping would let Savings quietly drift from reality — the same "two sources of truth" failure this whole conversation has been fighting elsewhere.
- **The catch\-up summary fires only after a real absence, not on every 1st (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** A plain message reports everything the catch\-up did, in both directions: *"Since you were last here: 6 deposits added €7,200, and 3 months of recurring expenses took €4,200."* Reporting only the deposits would tell someone €7,200 arrived while Savings sat €4,200 below what that implies — precisely the surprise this exists to prevent. The deductions are stated as plainly as the additions, with no verdict either way (§2.2).
  - **It is shown only when the catch\-up replayed a cycle falling wholly before the current calendar month.** Someone who used the app on 31 March and opens it on 1 April has technically had a cycle elapse while it was closed, and firing a summary at them would put a card in front of every user on the 1st of every month — a monthly nag in an app whose whole register is built against exactly that. A routine month\-boundary open produces no summary; a genuine multi\-month absence does.
  - **It renders as a dismissible card at the top of Budget home**, not a modal, and appears before §11.3's goal prompt if both are eligible on the same open.
  - **It persists until dismissed, tracked by `catchUpSummaryPending` (§12, added 2026\-09\-02).** Without a flag it could only render on the open that ran the catch\-up, so a person who backgrounds the app before reading it would never learn that €4,200 left Savings while they were away — which is the one thing the summary exists to tell them. §11.3's comparable once\-only prompt was given `goalPromptDismissed` for the same reason. **The card's figures are stored with the flag (schema 4, 2026\-09\-25, from the audit):** the flag alone survived a background but not a restart — the card rendered from memory, so a process death left the flag set, the card blank, and §11.3's prompt blocked behind it for good. `catchUpMonths`, `catchUpSwept`, `catchUpEnded` and the two deposit counters now hold what the card says; an unread card accumulates across later absences and is never cleared by an ordinary month boundary, only by its dismissal. §3.11's completion notices on an ordinary boundary are stored the same way.
- **No guard needed against auto\-deposit and a manual quick\-add landing in the same period.** If both happen, that's two real events, not a duplicate to detect (§2.3 — non\-blocking).
- **Two distinct trigger mechanisms, not one, despite sharing a catch\-up technique (Decided, 2026\-07\-28).** The calendar\-month boundary described above — Category reset and §3.11's Recurring Expense sweep — is one shared engine, firing on the 1st. Auto\-deposit's own catch\-up runs on a **separate** clock: the person's actual payday cadence (§3.3), tracked independently via `lastIncomeDepositDate` (§12) precisely because it can elapse on its own schedule. Both reuse the same underlying *technique* — compare a stored last\-processed marker against now, replay missed cycles with real historical dates — but they are two separate triggers, not one engine wearing two hats.
- **The two triggers are order\-independent, by construction (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Nothing either does depends on the other having run: Category reset touches only `spent`/`lastResetMonth`, the sweep touches only Savings and `rec:` rows, auto\-deposit touches only Savings and `savings` rows, and `incomeReceivedThisMonth` is derived from the ledger by date rather than incremented as deposits are processed (§3.3). This is deliberate — separate triggers that silently depend on an unstated running order aren't separate — and it is why §3.3 specifies that figure as derived rather than stored.
- **Catch\-up granularity differs by what's actually being caught up (Decided, 2026\-07\-28).** Recurring Expense sweeps and income deposits are genuinely iterated one real historical cycle at a time, each producing its own dated Transaction — a multi\-month gap never collapses into a single lump\-sum entry. **Category resets need no iteration:** since the reset writes no Transaction and moves nothing in Savings, skipping one month or six produces an identical end state, so the engine jumps straight there.
- **"New Month" notification (Decided, replaces §11.4's check\-in\-day concept entirely):** fires on the 1st of every calendar month, unconditionally — via real OS\-level scheduling on both target platforms (§1), surviving the app being unopened for any length of time and across a reboot, not an app\-open check. **Title:** "New Month." **Body:** "Fresh start — ready when you are." No numbers, no recap, no pressure to open the app. **Toggleable in Settings, default on** (§4.7) — even a deliberately gentle notification is still a notification, and nothing in this app should be non\-consensual (§2.3). §3.11's per\-expense due\-date reminders are scheduled through the same subsystem (§3.11, §4.7).
- ~~**Implementation is still open work, not yet built:**~~ **Built 2026\-09\-14 (§13.3); the plan as written:** the catch\-up logic only needs a session\-based check on app\-open. The notification side needs genuine platform\-level scheduling on Android *and* Windows 10/11 — permission handling where the platform has one, boot recovery, rescheduling as due dates advance — a real commitment, not just added copy. **This same trigger and catch\-up engine is also what §3.11's Recurring Expense sweep runs on.**

### 3\.10 Hourly income (~~Deferred — sketched, not built~~ designed with the owner and built 2026\-10\-26; stale justifications corrected 2026\-09\-02)

Income as amount\+cadence (§3.3) still assumes a *knowable* pay figure, which doesn't fit anyone paid by variable hours (retail, gig work, freelance\-adjacent hourly work) — for them, income only exists after the fact, once hours are actually worked.

Sketch, for whenever this is picked up:

- **Income gets a type — Salaried or Hourly** — not just a cadence. Salaried keeps the §3.3 amount\+cadence model.
- **Hourly asks for a rate (currency/hour), and nothing else is strictly required (revised 2026\-09\-02).** An "expected monthly hours" estimate was previously part of this sketch, justified solely by §3.5's threshold and §6.2's feasibility both needing *some* monthly rate to check against. **That justification is dead:** as of the 2026\-07\-23 reversal both read `incomeReceivedThisMonth`, a real running figure, and an hourly worker's actual earnings land in it exactly like anyone else's the moment they're logged through the Income button. Collecting the estimate anyway and wiring it into those checks would violate §3.3's explicit guardrail against `monthlyEquivalent()`\-style figures reaching Savings math. If an expected\-hours estimate is kept at all, its only remaining consumer is §10.2's Overtime calculator, and it should be justified there rather than here.
- **The Income "↓" quick\-add button** (**since 2026\-10\-09 the Savings sentence's *Income*, §3.6**) **becomes a small calculator** when Income is Hourly: hours worked × rate (rate pre\-filled from Settings, editable per entry so a differently\-paid shift doesn't need its own concept) → amount, shown before Confirm. **Scoped to the Income button specifically (revised 2026\-09\-02)** — this sketch predated the 2026\-07\-28 split of Savings quick\-add into two buttons and still said "quick\-add on Savings." It has to be the Income button: hours × rate is income by construction, and routing it through the general "\+" would leave every logged shift untagged and `incomeReceivedThisMonth` reading zero forever. The general "\+" keeps its plain amount field.
- A more honest, estimate\-free alternative — deriving a monthly rate from actual logged history instead of an upfront guess — isn't buildable yet, since it depends on the full transaction ledger (§7.3); the recurrence\-mechanism gap this section originally flagged has since been resolved (§3.9), so that dependency no longer applies, only the ledger one does.
- **The Hourly calculator's Transaction retains `hoursWorked` and `rate` as optional metadata, not just the computed amount (Decided, 2026\-07\-22).** Costs nothing to add now and is exactly the raw data the derive\-from\-history alternative above would need — without it, that alternative would stay blocked even once the ledger exists, since a flat amount can't be reverse\-engineered back into hours and rate.

**Designed with the owner and built 2026\-10\-26 (schema 9) — this replaces the sketch above where they differ.** Three problems the sketch left open were settled first. *Before or after tax:* hours × rate is pay before deductions, while the app counts pay as it lands (§3.3, §6.2), and for variable hours the share taken is not one stable figure a person could enter — so the person is never asked for a tax rate. *Per shift or per payday:* hours are logged whenever, but income appears only on payday, which keeps §3.3's rule that income counts when it lands. *The rest:* auto\-deposit and §3.3's *no income logged* note do not apply to hourly.

- **Settings → Income asks how the person is paid:** *A set amount* (salaried, §3.3 unchanged) or *By the hour* — a rate per hour and the same payday schedule (weekly, every two weeks, twice a month, monthly). One type per Profile; a second job is logged with the Income button as before. Hourly has no auto\-deposit, and switching to it starts counting paydays from today.
- **Logging hours:** the Savings sentence's *Income* becomes *[hours] h at €[rate] [day]* — the rate pre\-filled from Settings and editable per shift — and writes a **shift** (`shifts` table, §12) that moves nothing. Budget's Income section lists the shifts not yet paid, each editable or removable, under *"14.5 h logged for the payday on 13 Nov · about €290 before deductions"*, with a *Log hours* button.
- **Payday is a prompt that waits on Budget until answered** (`PaydayCard`): *"Payday, 13 Nov — 14.5 h logged. What arrived?"*, pre\-filled with the estimate. **Nothing lands until the person confirms**; the confirmed amount is income dated that payday, carrying the hours and the rate (§12's metadata), and the shifts it paid go. A payday covers **every shift dated on or before it**. With no hours logged it still asks. *Nothing this time* answers it without income; logged hours then wait for the next payday. The oldest unanswered payday is asked first.
- **The estimate is learned, not configured:** hours × rate, labelled *before deductions*, until the first payday is confirmed; after that, that figure scaled by what arrived against what was logged, **averaged over the last three paydays** (owner). The same rule fills §10.2's rate when Income is hourly.
- **Leaving hourly** — for salaried, or turning Income off — with hours still logged asks once: confirm what arrived for them now (dated today), or discard them. Nothing is left pending under a type that no longer uses it.
- **A payday notification** (§4.7's Notifications page, on by default, switchable) is sent **only while income is not recorded by itself** — hourly, or salaried without auto\-deposit: *"Payday — confirm what arrived"* or *"Payday — log what arrived"*. A tap opens the app, which on either platform has no way to land on a given screen, so the same result is reached from the app's side: hourly's prompt is already waiting on Budget, and for salaried by hand, **opening the app on the payday or the day after, with nothing logged since it, brings up the Income entry pre\-filled — once per payday.** Older misses stay with §3.3's note.
- **What the sketch had that the build does not:** the Income button as a calculator that writes income per shift (it would record money that has not arrived), an expected\-hours estimate (still unnecessary — the learned estimate replaces it), and the derive\-from\-history alternative as a separate idea (it is the learned estimate).


### 3\.11 Recurring Expenses: automatic monthly sweep (Decided, 2026\-07\-21; anchor chain and lifecycle specified 2026\-09\-02; creation branches and reminders built 2026\-09\-04)

Recurring Expenses are not manually logged occurrences — they're real, automatic claims against Savings, swept on schedule, closer in spirit to Income's auto\-deposit (§3.3) than to anything quick\-add ever covered. **Why sweeping early is more honest, not less (Decided, framing tightened 2026\-07\-23):** this money was never actually available for anything else in the first place — it's committed the moment the Recurring Expense exists, whether or not anyone has logged it yet. Showing it gone as soon as its cycle lands reflects that reality; leaving it sitting in Savings until someone remembers to log a payment would let the person see and feel access to money that was already spoken for, which is the false\-slack problem, not a neutral default. (Income gets the mirror\-image treatment for the same reason, in the opposite direction — see `incomeReceivedThisMonth`, §3.3.)

**Governing principle for this section and §3.3/§3.9 alike (Decided, 2026\-07\-28): model the real\-world event as closely as possible, everywhere except the 1st\-of\-month sweep trigger itself.** Auto\-deposit lands on the actual payday, never lumped to the 1st (§3.3); a Recurring Expense's real occurrence count is honored rather than averaged; the anchor chain and deletion\-reversal math follow real due dates rather than the bookkeeping trigger. The one deliberate exception is the 1st\-of\-month sweep timing itself — an administrative front\-loading choice in service of "this money was already spoken for," not a claim that every bill is literally due that day.

**Scope: obligations whose amount is knowable in advance and constant between discrete, knowable changes (Decided, 2026\-07\-23; refined 2026\-09\-02).** Rent, a subscription, a phone plan, a loan payment. A cost that varies *every cycle* — a grocery run, a usage\-based utility bill, a credit card's minimum payment (a percentage of a moving balance) — doesn't fit this entity and belongs in Categories (§3.2, §4.3), tracked as plan\-vs\-actual. **The refinement matters for §9: an obligation that is fixed for many cycles and then changes at a discrete, knowable moment — a variable\-rate loan between resets — is inside this scope, not outside it.** The dividing line was never "constant forever," it was "knowable in advance rather than discovered after the fact."

- **Cadence enum** is `weekly | biweekly | semimonthly | monthly | bimonthly | yearly` (§12) — mirroring Income's own cadence shape (§3.3), with `semimonthly` for expenses billed on two fixed calendar dates a month. Biweekly stays its own distinct cadence — a biweekly bill genuinely drifts across calendar dates over the year while true semimonthly billing never does.
- **A due date is required for every Recurring Expense, not gated behind reminders.** Cadence alone is only ever an interval — "monthly" doesn't say monthly *since when*. **Semimonthly requires a second due date (`dueDate2`),** mirroring Income's `incomePayDate`/`incomePayDate2` split (§3.3) exactly. **The editor shows the pair as Income shows its paydays (Decided 2026\-09\-25, with the owner):** *First due date* and *Second due date* are the month's two days in calendar order, each as its next date. The live `dueDate` alternates between the two days, so showing it under *First due date* had shown the second day once the first had passed. Saving writes the earlier date back as the next due date, and an untouched pair is not written at all.
- **A due date is "passed" from the start of the following day (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** An occurrence due today is still un\-elapsed: the anchor has not advanced, and the amount is still reversible on deletion. This boundary is hit constantly — a monthly fixed\-date expense due the 1st is swept on the same day it comes due — so leaving it to interpretation would swing the reversal prompt by a full occurrence for the app's most common configuration.
- **The due date entered at creation is preserved as `firstDueDate` (§12, added 2026\-09\-02), and no occurrence earlier than it is ever generated.** `dueDate` is normalised *forward* at creation (below), so counting the occurrences that land in a given calendar month requires walking the anchor chain backward as well as forward — and without a floor that walk invents occurrences from before the expense existed. Concretely: §9.1 deliberately sets a loan's first payment one month after origination, so a loan taken on 1 March has `dueDate` 1 April and anchor day 1; an unbounded backward walk would generate a 1 March occurrence and sweep €410 for a payment that was never owed. `firstDueDate` is the floor for every occurrence count, every threshold figure (§3.5), and every sweep.
- **Month\-end snapping is one shared rule, not a property of any single mode (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Wherever an occurrence is anchored to a calendar day a given month doesn't have, it lands on that month's last day — the 31st becomes the 30th, 29th or 28th as needed. This governs fixed\-date mode, semimonthly's two dates, and §3.3's monthly income anchor alike.
- **`dateMode` applies to `monthly`, `bimonthly` and `yearly` only (Decided, 2026\-07\-23; extended 2026\-09\-02).**
  - **Interval mode:** advances by a fixed day\-count each cycle, with no calendar\-day anchoring — drifts across months exactly as most real subscription contracts do. **The day\-count is stored as `intervalDays` (§12, added 2026\-09\-02), defaulting to 30 for `monthly`, 60 for `bimonthly` and 365 for `yearly`, and set once at creation.** It was previously described only as "a fixed day\-count," which is not a number: 30 vs 30.44 vs 31 changes whether a monthly interval expense produces twelve or thirteen occurrences a year, and a 30\-day interval can put two occurrences in one calendar month (1 January and 31 January), doubling that month's sweep. Weekly and biweekly need no such field — §3.3 already fixes them at 7 and 14 days.
  - **Fixed\-date mode:** advances to the same calendar day every cycle, snapped per the shared rule above. Never drifts.
  - It doesn't apply to `semimonthly`, which is inherently two fixed calendar dates. **By the identical argument it doesn't apply to `weekly` or `biweekly`\:** both are inherently intervals, and a week has no calendar day of the month, so `weekly` \+ `fixedDate` was a representable state whose two readings differ 4× in swept amount.
- **`dueDate` is a live, mutating anchor, and it advances when the due date actually passes — never when the sweep fires (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** The 1st\-of\-month sweep pre\-pays every occurrence landing in that month at once, so "advance when swept" and "advance when the real date passes" are two clocks that disagree for every cadence shorter than monthly. The governing principle settles it, and the deletion\-reversal rule already asserted it: the sweep trigger "is bookkeeping, not a real\-world event."
  - **Interval mode:** once the current `dueDate` passes, it advances by `intervalDays` and becomes the anchor for the next cycle — a rolling chain.
  - **Fixed\-date mode:** the next occurrence is the anchored calendar day in the following period, snapped for month length. **The anchored day is stored separately (`dueDateAnchorDay`, §12)** and never changes on its own. Without it, a rent anchored on the 31st snaps to 28 February, that snapped value becomes the new anchor, and every subsequent month recomputes from 28 — permanently relocating the due date, and firing its reminder three days early forever.
  - **Semimonthly** follows the same chain across its two dates: once `dueDate` passes it becomes the anchor and `dueDate2` becomes the next occurrence, and vice versa across the month boundary.
  - **A due date entered in the past is normalised at creation:** `dueDateAnchorDay` and `firstDueDate` are taken from the value the person entered, and `dueDate` is advanced by its cadence until it lands in the future. Past dates are explicitly acceptable input — the creation\-timing rules below contemplate them — but the anchor rule is phrased as an event that has already happened by then.
- **Optional reminder, off by default:** a preset lead time (`1 | 3 | 7 | 14` days, §12) counted back from the next occurrence. **These are real OS\-scheduled notifications, on the same subsystem as §3.9's New Month notification (Decided, 2026\-09\-02)** — rescheduled whenever `dueDate` advances, recovered across a reboot, and sharing the Settings → Notifications permission line (§4.7). An app\-open check would be useless: a reminder that only appears once you have already opened the app is not a reminder. Free\-form lead times are intentionally out of scope — a dedicated reminder app better serves anyone with more idiosyncratic needs.
  - **Built 2026\-09\-04 up to the platform seam.** The lead is counted back from `dueDate` — the live anchor — so the schedule follows the chain by construction: a 31st\-anchored rent fires on 25 February and returns to 28 March rather than staying on the 25th, which is the failure `dueDateAnchorDay` was added to prevent, expressed as a reminder rather than as a sweep. The registration is rebuilt from one function, called from every path that can change a due date, a reminder setting or the expense list, and restated at app open — which is also the boot\-recovery path (§13.3), since the OS holds no registration the app did not put there. ~~**`lastReminderFiredFor` (§12, added 2026\-09\-04)** makes firing once\-per\-occurrence a property of state rather than of a callback, so a reboot's re\-registration does not re\-fire what already fired, and an advanced anchor re\-arms the reminder by itself.~~ **The fire time is the guard (Decided, 2026\-09\-25, with the owner):** a reminder stays registered while its 09:00 is still ahead and is dropped once it has passed, so no re\-sync — an open, a write, a reboot, the daily top\-up — can re\-fire one, and an advanced anchor re\-arms it by itself. `lastReminderFiredFor` was never written by anything and could not be on Windows, where a scheduled toast fires with no process of the app alive; it is removed (schema 4). The date filter it stood beside had cancelled any reminder synced earlier on its own fire day. **The same instant decides §3.9's New Month ping:** this month's 1st while its 09:00 is ahead, else next month's — the build had re\-armed from next month on every sync, so the Windows 08:30 top\-up cancelled every ping. **The top\-up re\-arms from state as it stands that day:** the anchor chain is advanced in memory first, or an unopened app registered nothing after the first reminder passed. Copy is a plain statement of the item and its figure — *"Phone bill — €45 · due Sep 7"*. **One formatter for screens and notifications (Decided, 2026\-09\-25, with the owner):** every amount is shown as it is — *€950.40*, with *.00* dropped from a whole amount — and dates are month\-first; the screens had rounded every amount to whole units and the notifications printed exact cents day\-first. An unknown currency code shows the code itself (*CHF 9.99*), never another currency's symbol — with no imperative and no urgency (§2.2, §2.4). When the OS permission is denied nothing displays while the schedule and the person's own settings stay exactly as they were, which is the state §4.7's plain line reports.

**Sweep mechanism — one shared rule, not a per\-cadence special case:**

- **Cadences of one month or less** (weekly, biweekly, semimonthly, monthly): on the 1st of each calendar month, count how many real occurrences' due dates land within that calendar month — never earlier than `firstDueDate` — and deduct their full combined face value from Savings. Deliberately a real count, not an average — a biweekly expense debits twice most months and three times occasionally, and the app reflects that honestly.
- **Cadences longer than a month** (bimonthly, yearly): apportioned evenly per month instead — a smoothed sinking\-fund contribution rather than one lump deduction landing in a single month.
  - **The share is `amount ÷ the number of 1st-of-month sweep dates falling after now and on or before the next due date`, computed once and held for the cycle (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** It is stored as `apportionedShare` (§12) and recomputed when `dueDate` advances, at creation, **and on any confirmed edit that changes `amount`, `cadence`, `dueDate` or `endDate`** — an edit is a new plan, and leaving the old share in place until the next due date could hold a stale figure for up to a year. Recomputing it every month from a shrinking divisor, by contrast, would make the share *grow* — €1,200/12, then /11, then /10 — which is the opposite of holding.
  - **Worked, to fix the count.** A settled yearly bill whose due date has just passed on 15 July 2026, next due 15 July 2027: the sweeps falling after today and on or before that date are 1 Aug 2026 through 1 Jul 2027 — **twelve**, giving the expected €100/month. The same bill **created on 5 March 2026** with a first due date of 15 July 2026: the qualifying sweeps are 1 Apr, 1 May, 1 Jun and 1 Jul — **four**, giving **€300/month**, so the full €1,200 is reserved before the real charge lands. A bimonthly expense due 15 March with the next due 15 May gives two sweeps, the expected half.
  - **The month containing the due date is the closing cycle's last sweep, and the new cycle starts on the following 1st.** In the example above, 1 July sweeps the fourth and final €300 of the old cycle; the due date passes on 15 July, the share is recomputed to €100, and the new cycle's first sweep is 1 August. Nothing sweeps out of band on the due date itself.
- **Each real occurrence writes its own Transaction (Decided, 2026\-09\-02; built 2026\-09\-04)** — `key: rec:<id>`, `amount` positive per §3.6's sign table, dated to the occurrence's own real due date, not to the 1st. A biweekly bill swept for two occurrences on 1 March writes two rows dated to the two real due dates. This is what §5.1's FIFO walk and §5.3's history need to place consumption where it really happened, and what lets a partial deletion reversal net exactly against the occurrence it reverses. For apportioned cadences the row is dated to the 1st it swept on, since no single real occurrence corresponds to it.
- **Reuses the same New Month trigger and missed\-cycle catch\-up logic §3.9 defines for Categories** — fires on the 1st, and if the app was closed across multiple cycles, **replays every missed cycle individually with its real historical date** rather than collapsing them or skipping to the current month.
  - **The trigger is opening the app, and the anchor chain runs on every open, not only when a month boundary is crossed (clarified 2026\-09\-04, from building it).** §3.9 says the trigger is the app opening; a build that ran the chain only from the month\-boundary path left every `dueDate` stale after a few days away — no month had turned, so nothing advanced — and the reminders registered against dates that had already passed. Running it at open is idempotent: on an ordinary open it moves no money and only restates the schedule.
- **§3.5's recurring\-expense threshold and §6.2's goal feasibility both read the month's scheduled occurrence total** — computed by the same count/apportionment math, whether or not the sweep has fired yet.

**Termination (`endDate`, nullable — Decided, 2026\-07\-23; comparison and finished state specified 2026\-09\-02):** most Recurring Expenses run indefinitely, but some have a known, finite life — a Loan's repayment schedule (§9.1) being the clearest case.

- **An occurrence sweeps if and only if its own due date falls on or before `endDate`.** The comparison is due\-date\-to\-`endDate`, never sweep\-date\-to\-`endDate` — the sweep fires on the 1st and `endDate` is a due date, so comparing the two produced three different answers for the same loan depending on which an engineer picked. The first occurrence whose due date falls after `endDate` doesn't sweep, and the entity finishes at that point.
- **An auto\-updating Loan's expense also finishes the moment its Account balance reaches zero, whatever `endDate` says (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** `endDate` is fixed at origination, so an early payoff would otherwise leave the expense sweeping to the original term — taking real money out of Savings every month for a debt that no longer exists, while §7.2's decrement, floored at zero, has nothing left to reduce and the two figures drift apart. Finishing on balance \= 0 also lets §3.11's completion notice and §5.3's strikethrough fire when they are actually true rather than years later.
- **"Partial" applies only to apportioned cadences.** For cadences of a month or less every occurrence is full face value. For bimonthly and yearly, a cycle cut short sweeps `apportionedShare` for the months actually elapsed in that cycle and no more.
- **Finished\-ness is recorded, not re\-derived (`finishedAt`, nullable timestamp, §12).** §3.11 rightly refuses a second, independently\-decrementing *occurrence counter* — that would be a competing source of truth. A terminal marker is a different thing: it records that an event happened, the same role `lastSweptMonth` plays, and §3.9's catch\-up needs it to know whether a multi\-month replay has already passed the final occurrence. `dueDate` stops advancing once it is set. **Who sets it at `endDate` (clarified 2026\-09\-25, from the audit):** the month boundary, never the anchor chain. The chain stops at the last occurrence on or before `endDate`; §3.9's replay finishes the expense on the first 1st on or after it, after that month's sweep. Marking it from the chain — which runs first on every open — made the replay skip the final sweeps, the completion notice and a Loan's closing row whenever the app was opened between the last occurrence and the next 1st.
- On finishing, a plain, non\-graded notice — *"Loan paid off — this Recurring Expense has ended"* (§2.2). **Where it shows (Decided, 2026\-09\-14): as a line on §3.9's "Since you were last here" card, in the app — not as an OS notification.** **§9.1's interest\-only and variable\-rate cases carry different copy**, since neither is reliably "paid off" at its `endDate`.
- **Once finished, the title displays with a strikethrough** — a plain cue that it's no longer active, distinct from deleting it. Deleting a finished entry shows no reversal prompt: its obligation is discharged, not interrupted.

**Creation timing — four branches (Decided, 2026\-07\-21; apportioned and later\-month branches added 2026\-09\-02):**

- **Cadences of a month or less, first due date still ahead in the current month:** sweeps immediately on creation (same count math as any other sweep), then resumes the 1st\-of\-month rhythm.
- **Cadences of a month or less, first due date already passed earlier in the current month:** no catch\-up sweep; the first real sweep is the next 1st.
- **Cadences of a month or less, first due date in a later month:** never sweeps on creation, and nothing is swept for the current month, since no occurrence falls in it. This is the ordinary case for a subscription added on the 25th that first bills on the 5th, and for §9.1's loan payments, which are deliberately dated one month after origination — it matched none of the earlier branches, which left `lastSweptMonth` unset and the next app\-open free to sweep a month that had no occurrence in it.
- **Apportioned cadences (bimonthly, yearly):** never sweep on creation. The first share sweeps on the next 1st, which is exactly what the share formula above counts. An earlier draft of this section said such an expense also swept its creation month, which over\-reserved by one full share.
- **All four branches set `lastSweptMonth` to the creation month.** The immediate\-sweep branch because it *is* that month's sweep; the other three because that month is deliberately skipped. Left unset, the next app\-open finds an un\-swept current month and sweeps it — an €800 expense created on the 20th debiting twice by the 22nd.

**Built 2026\-09\-04, with one ambiguity resolved and one rule that had to be built first.**

- **The branch is selected on the first due date, not on whether some later occurrence in the month is still ahead.** All three short\-cadence branches above are phrased that way, and branch 2's entire point is that it does not back\-sweep — a biweekly expense first due on the 5th and created on the 20th takes branch 2 even though its 26th is still ahead.
- **A first due date in an *earlier* month is folded into branch 2.** The four branches name "already passed earlier in the current month" and "in a later month" but not "in an earlier month", which is representable, since past dates are explicitly acceptable input and `dueDate` is normalised forward at creation. It is folded into branch 2 because "passed" is "passed", and the alternative — back\-sweeping a month the expense did not exist for — is the phantom charge `firstDueDate`'s floor exists to prevent. Recorded here rather than left to an implementer to rediscover.
- **Branch 3 was unbuildable until the occurrence walk became real.** "Nothing is swept for the current month, since no occurrence falls in it" is a statement about occurrences, and the count was a table of constants for three of the four short cadences — semimonthly always 2, monthly always 1, with a real walk only for weekly and biweekly. So `firstDueDate` and `endDate` were honoured for two cadences out of four, `dueDate2` was stored and read by nothing at all, and a monthly expense first due in November swept in September. One shared walk now generates the real occurrence dates for every cadence of a month or less, floored at `firstDueDate` and capped at `endDate` — which is also what makes `endDate`'s "if and only if its own due date falls on or before `endDate`" a per\-occurrence comparison rather than a per\-month one.
- **`dateMode` is now unrepresentable on the three cadences it cannot apply to.** The editor offered it for weekly and biweekly, so `weekly` \+ `fixedDate` — the state this section calls out as differing 4× in swept amount between its two readings — was reachable in two taps.

**Editing an already\-swept Recurring Expense: reconcile once, then apply forward (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Editing `amount`, `cadence`, `dueDate`, `dueDate2`, `dateMode` or `endDate` after the current month's sweep has fired leaves Savings holding a figure computed from the old settings. Rather than silently ignore that or silently move money:

- The app recomputes what the current month's scheduled total *would* be under the new settings and, if it differs, shows a one\-time prompt with the difference — *"Return €200 to Savings?"* for a decrease, *"Take a further €200 from Savings?"* for an increase — with the usual Confirm/Cancel pair and no default answer implied. Confirming applies the difference and logs a Transaction (`key: rec:<id>`), **dated to the occurrence it adjusts**, so the "sum of un\-elapsed `rec:` Transactions" reversal test can still be applied to it. Cancelling leaves the month as swept.
- **On a Loan\-linked expense, a confirmed difference also adjusts `Account.balance` by the same amount as principal, and logs its own `acct:` Transaction dated to the same occurrence (added 2026\-09\-02).** The adjustment follows the direction of the Savings movement: a further €200 taken from Savings reduces the tracked debt by €200, and €200 returned to Savings restores it. §7.2's decrement is keyed to sweeps, so without this the tracked debt drifts by every rate reset's difference — the exact failure §7.2's rate\-field linkage was added to prevent.
- **Either way, the new settings govern from the next sweep onward.** This is the one moment the person is offered the choice, deliberately: it keeps a mistyped digit from instantly moving real money the way an automatic re\-sweep would, while refusing to leave the current month knowingly wrong with no way to say so. It is also what makes a variable\-rate loan's annual reset (§9.1) a two\-tap correction rather than a silent drift.

**Deletion reversal — governed by the same anchor/next\-occurrence chain (Decided, 2026\-07\-21; mechanics 2026\-07\-28; amount source corrected 2026\-09\-02):** deleting a Recurring Expense **before its next occurrence** triggers an unskippable prompt — *"Add €\[amount\] back to Savings?"* — offering back whatever has been swept for the current, not\-yet\-due period, and logging a Transaction (`key: rec:<id>`, negative amount) when accepted. Without that Transaction the ledger would show a sweep out with no corresponding return, and §5.1's and §5.3's figures would sit permanently out of step with Savings.

- **The amount offered is what the ledger recorded — the sum of that expense's un\-elapsed `rec:<id>` Transactions — never the entity's current `amount` field.** These come apart the moment anything is edited: rent swept at €1,000 and edited to €1,200 would otherwise offer €1,200 back, a €200 credit for money that never left.
- **The prompt appears only when that amount is greater than zero.** An expense created on the 20th with a due date of the 10th sweeps nothing, so deleting it on the 25th has nothing to reverse — the same reasoning that suppresses the prompt for a finished expense.
- **Cadences of a month or less:** the amount swept for the current occurrence, regardless of whether the 1st\-of\-month trigger has fired.
  - **Cadences with more than one due date inside a swept month — semimonthly always, biweekly on its 2–3\-occurrence months — follow the identical rule per occurrence, not an all\-or\-nothing reversal.** Worked example (semimonthly, due the 15th and 30th, €300 each): deleted before the 15th → both halves are ahead of their due dates, €600 returns. Deleted on the 20th → the 15th has passed and become the anchor, so only the 30th's €300 returns. Deleted after the 30th → nothing from the current month is reversible. Because each occurrence wrote its own dated Transaction, this is a filter over real rows rather than a fraction computed on the side.
- **Apportioned cadences:** the shares swept so far in the current cycle — four months at €300 returns €1,200, read from the ledger like every other case, which is what keeps it correct after a mid\-cycle creation has changed the share. The cycle's rows are the ones dated **after the previous occurrence**, which follows from this section's own "the month containing the due date is the closing cycle's last sweep, and the new cycle starts on the following 1st."

**Built 2026\-09\-04 — and the reversal could not be built until the rows were.** A month used to write one row per expense, for the combined amount, dated to the 1st. Three of this section's rules were unimplementable against that shape, and one was quietly wrong:

- The reversal is specified as **"the sum of that expense's un\-elapsed `rec:<id>` Transactions"** and was instead reading the entity through the month's sweep amount — the exact substitution the next bullet forbids. With one undated lump there was nothing to filter: it returned a whole month or nothing.
- **Semimonthly and biweekly reversals are per occurrence**, and this section's own worked example — due the 15th and 30th at €300, deleted on the 20th, €300 returns — had no rows to express it with.
- §5.1's FIFO walk and §5.3's history place consumption where it happened, and every occurrence sat on the 1st.
- **The edit\-and\-reconcile row was dated to the 1st of the month rather than to the occurrence it adjusts**, defeating the reason this section gives for dating it that way ("so the 'sum of un\-elapsed `rec:` Transactions' reversal test can still be applied to it"). A monthly expense due the 20th, edited on the 10th, put the adjustment on an already\-elapsed 1st while the occurrence it corrected was still reversible on the 20th — so deleting returned the original sweep and silently dropped the adjustment. It is now dated to the earliest still\-reversible occurrence in the month, falling back to the month's last.

**The share formula is built (2026\-09\-04), and it needed a second half that was not obvious.** It had been computed as `amount ÷ cycle length` — the *average* monthly cost, not a sinking\-fund contribution — which under\-reserved every mid\-cycle creation: €400 of a €1,200 bill set aside by the day it landed. Two things had to change together, and the first without the second is worse than the original:

- **The formula**, as written above and verified against all three worked examples in it.
- **The recompute when `dueDate` advances**, which was never built. The flat divisor hid it, because `amount ÷ 12` is the same number every cycle. With the real formula and no recompute, the first cycle's €300 is held forever: **12 × €300 = €3,600 against a €1,200 bill, permanently.** The old bug was bounded and one\-off; that one would have been unbounded and recurring.
- **An edit that changes none of the listed fields is not a new plan, and the held share stands.** The corollary was not being honoured: the share was recomputed on every save, so renaming an expense, changing its icon or toggling its reminder re\-derived it from a shorter window — a settled €600 yearly at €50 a month became €100 a month because someone fixed a typo in its name. The trigger is the plan fields moving, not the editor closing.
- **The recompute measures from the moment the cycle began — the previous occurrence — not from today.** Measuring from today is the shrinking\-divisor growth this section already rejects, and it also breaks catch\-up: an anchor that rolls over while the app is closed would otherwise be recomputed against a compressed window and produce a share that cycle never should have had. Measured from the previous occurrence, a late catch\-up yields the same figure a live rollover would have.

**The divisor floors at 1 (added 2026\-09\-04 — this section does not cover the case).** An expense created on 20 June and first due on the 25th has no 1st\-of\-month sweep between the two, and creation branch 4 does not sweep on creation, so the count is zero and the division undefined. Floored, the whole amount sweeps on the following 1st — after the charge rather than before it, which is late but honest, and the next cycle is correct. The alternative, sweeping the full amount at creation, would contradict branch 4 for the sake of an edge case.
