So Savings vs. Owed compares the money that is genuinely free against the money that is genuinely owed. It is not a net\-worth figure and does not try to be one — which is also why it no longer carries that name.

**History is ledger\-derived, not snapshot\-based (Decided, 2026\-07\-28) — and is now actually derivable (closed 2026\-09\-02).** Account edits, quick\-add and direct alike, log Transactions of their own (§3.6, §7.1) even though they never move Savings. That was several Transactions short of sufficient, because the ledger recorded only *deltas* and nothing recorded an opening or closing balance — so a forward replay from zero showed a €14,000 loan as €0 for every month it existed, and a backward walk back\-projected it into months before it was created, which this same paragraph forbids. Four writes close it:

- **Creating an Account with a non\-zero balance logs an opening Transaction** (`key: acct:<id>`, `amount` \= the entered balance, dated to creation).
- **The first *positive* value of `Savings.amount` logs its opening lot** (§3.1, §5.1). A deliberate €0 opens none — there is nothing to date — so the lot arrives with the first real money.
- **Loan auto\-update decrements log their own Transactions** (§7.2) — without which the report is exactly wrong for the one user its existence was justified by, someone actively paying down a loan.
- **Deleting an Account logs a closing Transaction** (`key: acct:<id>`, `amount` \= the negative of its balance at deletion, dated to the deletion), **added 2026\-09\-02.** Without it, a forward replay keeps subtracting a paid\-off\-and\-deleted loan's residual balance in every month after it was deleted — so someone who clears a €7,000 loan and removes it would see their position permanently €7,000 worse than it is, which is the exact opposite of what the report is for.

With those four, a forward replay from zero is exact for both series, a deleted Account stays reconstructible from its surviving rows and their `targetLabel` snapshots, and it correctly contributes nothing after its closing row.

Follows the same real\-life\-only principle §3.11's governing rule establishes: a newly created Account contributes nothing to the trend for months before it existed, and deleting one changes nothing about the history already shown for the months it was live. **"End of life" means a Loan whose linked Recurring Expense has finished (Decided, 2026\-09\-02)** — such a Loan shows the strikethrough treatment matching a finished Recurring Expense's (§3.11) until the person deletes it. The trigger is §3.11's `finishedAt` on the linked expense, reached via `linkedRecurringExpenseId`, so no new field is introduced. **Credit accounts have no end of life and never show the treatment** — they are revolving by construction (§9.2, "no fixed principal or term"), and the obvious guess of "balance reached zero" would strike through the live, healthy card of someone who pays in full every cycle, turning §9.2's explicitly favoured behaviour into an implied verdict.

**Enabling the toggle for the first time shows the full, real backfilled history immediately** — the underlying Savings and Account activity already existed before anyone switched the report on, so there's no reason to start the trend from a blank slate.

### 5\.4 Income vs Expenses (Decided; scope and series specified 2026\-09\-02)

**Shown only when Income is enabled (§4.6, Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** With Income off there is nothing to put on the income side: `isIncome` is set only by auto\-deposit or the "↓" button (the Savings sentence's *Income* since 2026\-10\-09, §3.6) (§12), neither of which exists in that state, so the report would draw a permanent flat zero line beside a real expense series — the "hole where income should be" §3.3 explicitly forbids. The alternative considered was redefining the income series as *all* Savings\-increasing Transactions so every user gets the report; it was rejected because it would make "Income" mean something broader here than the `isIncome` flag means everywhere else in the document, and because a birthday gift or a friend repaying a loan is Savings, not income. The app is meant to fit different real situations rather than show every feature to everyone regardless of fit.

- **Income series:** the sum of `isIncome` Transactions per month.
- **Expenses series:** the sum of Savings\-decreasing Transactions per month, excluding Goal contributions — a goal contribution is money kept rather than money spent, and putting it on the expense side of an income\-vs\-expenses comparison would misdescribe it. (This is a deliberate difference from §5.2, which is a complete outflow breakdown and does include Goals as their own slice; the two reports answer different questions.) **Goal money counts once, when it is spent from the Goal (Decided 2026\-10\-10, with the owner):** a `spend:` row (§3.6) leaves Savings untouched, so the Savings\-decreasing sum never saw it and a €80 hotel paid from Holidays was missing from the report; it now counts in its month at its amount. The contribution stays excluded, so the money is not counted twice.
- **Window:** ~~six months, matching §5.1 and §5.3,~~ **`trendMonths` months (§4.6, default 6; corrected 2026\-09\-25),** attributed by Transaction `date` (§3.6).

One accent at two opacities distinguishes the two series — never a red/green pair, since income and expenses aren't a "good/bad" pair, just two different things.

**A headline, like the other three reports (Decided, 2026\-09\-15):** the current month's two figures — *"€2,400 in · €2,774 out"* — in the same card the Buffer, Spending and Owed views open with; `incomeHeadline()` makes the line. The explanatory note about the two opacities sits **under** the chart, not above it: an explanation follows the thing it explains. No value axis on the bars — the headline carries the numbers.

## 6\. Goals

### 6\.1 Two\-mode calculator (Decided; arithmetic specified 2026\-09\-02)

**By a date** (person picks a timeline, app suggests a monthly contribution) or **by an amount** (person picks a contribution, app suggests a timeline) — kept in sync with each other as the person adjusts either.

**The arithmetic, stated (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — the one piece of maths in §6 was previously given without any of the parameters needed to compute it):**

- **The amount divided is the amount still needed: `target − saved`.** §6.3 seeds `saved` at creation as "optional amount already saved," and §12 makes it the goal's running tally, so this is the only reading under which "kept in sync" stays true as `saved` grows. A €1,200 goal with €400 already saved over six months is €133.33/month, not €200 — a 50% difference in the figure §6.2 and §6.4 then read against.
- **Months are counted as calendar months from the current month to the target month**, per §3.9's single period definition.
- **By\-date rounds the monthly figure up** to the currency's minor unit; rounding down produces a rate that provably misses the date. **By\-amount rounds the resulting timeline up** to the next whole month and displays the resulting target month, for the same reason.
- `monthly` and `months` are derived from `targetDate` and re\-derived whenever `saved`, `target` or `targetDate` changes (§12) — never stored as a standalone duration that silently decays as time passes.

### 6\.2 Non\-blocking feasibility check (Decided; income side corrected 2026\-07\-23; completed\-goal rule and rendering added 2026\-09\-02)

Compares a goal's required monthly contribution against **income received so far this month (`incomeReceivedThisMonth`, §3.3) minus this month's scheduled recurring\-expense total (§3.11) minus every other goal's monthly commitment** — deliberately excluding day\-to\-day Category spending (which stays the person's own flexible room) and, per §3.5's reasoning, excluding Savings itself (a large cushion shouldn't be able to silently mask a monthly rate that doesn't actually add up). Never blocks saving an "infeasible" plan — the person may know something the app doesn't.

**A Goal stops counting toward "every other goal's monthly commitment" once `saved ≥ target` (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Nothing previously defined when a commitment ended, so a person who finished three €200/month goals last year would have €600 of phantom commitments subtracted forever, and every goal they created from then on would read as unaffordable — in an app whose entire register is built on not discouraging people. Excluding a reached goal is pure arithmetic, not a verdict, so it doesn't reopen §2.2's no\-grading rule.

- **Reaching the target does not close the Goal.** The person can keep adding to it afterward, and each such contribution is a real transfer against Savings like any other (§3.2). What stops is only the *forward* commitment this check subtracts — the plan is complete, so there is no further monthly rate to reserve.
- **A Goal whose target date has passed while `saved < target` keeps counting.** The target wasn't reached, so the intention presumably stands; dropping it would quietly inflate available room, which is the direction §3.2's conservatism rules out.
- Goals with a null `monthly` (§6.3) never counted in the first place — there is no rate to subtract.

**What the check renders (specified 2026\-09\-02):** the goal's required monthly figure and the remaining room, side by side as two plain figures, with no ratio, no runway figure, and no verdict line. §6.4's Income\-off variant compares a monthly rate against a Savings stock and explicitly forbids dividing them, which leaves side\-by\-side presentation as the only coherent rendering; stating it here keeps one engineer from shipping a bare pair of numbers and another from inventing a months\-of\-runway division.

**A predictable consequence, carried across from §3.5 (2026\-09\-02):** for an Income\-on person the remaining room is reliably negative for the first days of most months, since the full month's recurring commitment is counted from the 1st while paychecks arrive gradually. §3.5 already documents and accepts this for its own threshold; it applies identically here, and to §6.4's always\-present frame view. It is honest cyclical behaviour, not a bug, and no smoothing is applied to hide it.

### 6\.3 No\-target Goal mode (Decided, extended 2026\-07\-20; ~~not yet built~~ built — `GoalEditing.kt`'s `noTarget`)

A Goal can be created **without a target amount, without an end date, or without either** — tracked, contributed to, visible, but never marked "achieved." Confirmed to apply to Emergency Fund goals as well as the general "second savings pool, tracked separately, never spent" case described in §8.2. This is the mechanism a second conceptual "savings account" should use, rather than a special\-cased account type.

The minimal path is: **Create Goal → Name, optional amount already saved → Add.** No maximum amount, no required end date. With no target and no date, there is nothing to divide by — no suggested monthly rate is computed, and no feasibility read is shown — the goal exists purely as a name and a running tally, addable to $1 at a time, so a person can see it, want it, and make progress toward it regardless of their current situation. A **date\-only Goal (a deadline with no target amount) is explicitly out of scope** — it doesn't correspond to anything meaningful to compute or display.

### 6\.4 Calculator criteria (Decided, 2026\-07\-20; threshold base corrected 2026\-09\-02)

The original problem: the two\-mode calculator (§6.1) was pure arithmetic with nothing behind it, so by\-date and by\-amount produced a suggestion regardless of whether it was achievable — a goal could be set that consumed 40% of a budget already running near its limit, with nothing surfacing that fact at the moment it mattered. The fix keeps §6.1's math universally available (nobody is ever blocked from creating a goal, per §2.3/§6.3) while adding two separate, differently\-triggered contextual views inside the calculator, before confirmation:

- **"Where this fits in your current frame"** — a collapsed view, Buffer\-style (§5.1), **always present** once any monthly figure is computed, regardless of whether anything below is triggered. Shows §6.2's feasibility read, rendered as §6.2 specifies:
  
  - **Income on:** income received this month minus scheduled recurring expenses minus other goals' monthly commitments.
  - **Income off:** the income term is dropped, not zeroed\-and\-shown — reads as recurring expenses plus other goals' commitments weighed against Savings alone. A plain comparison, not a division — displays correctly even when Savings is zero or negative (§3.1), and is hidden entirely while `Savings.amount` is `null`.
  
  This is pure context, shown even when the number looks completely fine — the same reason Buffer itself is always visible rather than appearing only when something looks wrong.

- **"If you'd like to make this more sustainable, here are a few options"** — a second, separately\-collapsed view, **conditional**\: appears only when the computed monthly figure exceeds a sustainability threshold. **The threshold's base is income when Income is on, and Savings when it isn't (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — corrects a base that was keyed to the toggle rather than to real money):**
  
  - **Income on: 1/6 of `incomeReceivedThisMonth`.**
  - **Income off: 1/12 of Savings.**
  
  Previously both fractions were taken of *Savings*, switched by whether `incomeEnabled` was true — which doubled a person's allowance on the strength of a paycheck that hadn't landed, the exact error the 2026\-07\-23 pass rewrote §3.3, §3.5 and §6.2 to eliminate everywhere else. §6.4 was the one Savings\-facing check that never got the correction, and it produced opposite advice for two people holding identical money. The corrected shape mirrors §3.5 exactly: income base when income exists, Savings base when it doesn't.
  
  When it fires, it offers **3 non\-mandatory suggested amounts**, each shown with its corresponding timeline, so the person can feel the amount/time trade\-off rather than being told a number is simply too high. The suggestions take fractions of the same base as the trigger:
  
  - **Income on:** 1/14, 1/12, 1/6 of `incomeReceivedThisMonth`.
  - **Income off:** 1/20, 1/16, 1/12 of Savings (the tighter set reflecting that an Income\-off pool has no replenishment).
  
  Picking a suggestion re\-populates the calculator's fields (still fully editable); ignoring them and confirming the original number works exactly as before. Nothing here blocks — consistent with §2.3/§6.2. This is a deliberately different treatment from the recurring\-expense threshold (§3.5): a recurring expense can only be flagged, since the person doesn't control it beyond "have it, don't have it" — a Goal is something the person actively sets, so the calculator can offer a reshaped alternative instead of only a note.
  
  - **When the base is ≤ 0, the trigger still fires unconditionally — correctly — but the three amounts break down** (at a base of 0 all three compute to €0; below 0 they compute to negative contributions). **Fix:** suppress the numeric suggestions and show a single plain\-fact line instead, in the same non\-judgmental register as everywhere else (§2.2) — *"there's no Savings cushion right now to suggest alternative amounts from"* with Income off, or *"no income has landed yet this month to suggest alternative amounts from"* with Income on. The trigger condition and the frame view above are unaffected; only the suggestion content changes.
  - **With Income on, a base of €0 is the normal state at the start of every month** (§3.5's documented lumpiness), so the plain\-fact line will be what an Income\-on person sees on the 1st and the trigger will fire on any positive figure. This is the same honest cyclical behaviour §3.5 and §6.2 already accept, surfacing here too rather than being smoothed away.

- These two views are independent of each other — the frame view never depends on the sustainability check firing, and the sustainability view never suppresses or replaces the frame view.

- **Goals with no computed monthly figure sit outside this system entirely** — no frame view, no sustainability check, since there is nothing to compare. **This is the test, rather than "no\-date, amount\-only" (corrected 2026\-09\-02):** that older phrasing read equally onto §6.1's *by\-amount mode* — which has no date but very much does produce a monthly figure, and is precisely the mode where the person picks a contribution the sustainability check exists to react to. The rule is §6.3's own: any Goal whose `monthly` is null is outside both views; any Goal with a `monthly`, in either §6.1 mode, gets both.

## 7\. Accounts

### 7\.1 Scope (Decided, revised 2026\-07\-23 — Checking and Cash removed)

**Accounts: Credit and Loan only** — a single flat list, the earlier Everyday/Tracking split retired along with the types it existed to separate. (Investment removed — see §3.4.) **Checking and Cash are gone entirely, not merged into Savings (Decided, 2026\-07\-23):** they were structurally redundant with mechanisms the app already has. A second, separately\-tracked cash\-like balance either duplicates Savings itself (§3.1) or duplicates what Profiles exist to do (§8) if it's genuinely someone else's money — either way, keeping them would recreate exactly the double\-counting risk this app has otherwise been careful to avoid. This is the same move already made once for "Not yet assigned" (§3.7): retiring a redundant concept outright rather than managing confusion around it with guidance or a disclaimer.

Both remaining Account types are liabilities, a genuinely different kind of quantity from Savings' asset pool — a Credit or Loan balance can't double\-count against an asset the way two cash\-trackers could, since Savings vs. Owed explicitly subtracts them (§5.3) rather than adding them alongside Savings.

**Accounts are informational and Savings\-vs\-Owed\-only by default — no Account balance feeds Savings, Goal or Category math. There are two exceptions, both of which run the other way (an Account's own screen causing a Savings movement, never the reverse):**

- **Loan auto\-update (Decided, 2026\-07\-23):** when its optional toggle is on (§7.2, §9.1), a Loan's balance is decremented automatically as its linked Recurring Expense sweeps. This never touches Savings math itself — it only keeps the Loan's own tracked figure honest.
- **A Credit Account's payoff\-outlook panel can create a Recurring Expense (§9.2, added 2026\-09\-02).** Where the person commits to a chosen fixed monthly payment, that expense sweeps Savings on the normal schedule like any other. This is the one place an Account screen sets up a real, recurring Savings movement, and it is why the panel is better described as read\-only *with respect to other math* than as taking no action: it feeds nothing, but it can create something.

**Credit Accounts host the payoff\-outlook panel** (§9.2, Surface 1) directly on the Account's own screen — live context computed from the account's real balance and stored `apr`, feeding no other figure in the app.

### 7\.2 Full CRUD (Decided, implemented; Loan auto\-update added 2026\-07\-23; inputs, retype and linkage specified 2026\-09\-02)

Add, rename, retype, delete, and directly edit balance — every Account is a fully editable item, consistent with §3.6's editability principle. Quick\-add remains available as a standalone balance nudge, with the explicit direction control §3.6 specifies (Charge/Payment on Credit, Payment/Increase on Loan). **Creating an Account with a non\-zero balance logs an opening Transaction, and deleting one logs a closing Transaction** (§5.3), without which the account's history is unreconstructible.

**A Payment quick\-add on an Account offers the matching Savings outflow in the same confirm step (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** For a swept loan payment the app produces both halves automatically — a `rec:` row moving Savings and an `acct:` row moving the balance. A lump\-sum payment made by hand had no equivalent: because an Account quick\-add never touches Savings (§7.1), someone paying €7,000 off a loan would reduce the tracked debt while Buffer, Spending and Age of Money never noticed the €7,000 that actually left their bank account, unless they independently remembered to log a second entry nothing told them about. The confirm step now writes both rows.

- **It defaults to ON for an Account with no linked Recurring Expense**, which is the hand\-paid case this exists for.
- **It defaults to OFF when the Account has a `linkedRecurringExpenseId`**, because that expense's sweep has already moved the Savings side for the period. §9.2's Credit fixed payment is the case that bites: the card carries no auto\-update, so the person must log the €200 payment by hand to keep `balance` honest — and following an on\-by\-default prompt would duplicate €200 of outflow into Buffer, Spending and Age of Money every single month, with nothing on screen to suggest they should uncheck it.
- Either default can be overridden per payment, for the ordinary case of a payment that genuinely didn't come from tracked Savings, or an extra payment on top of a swept one.

**Loan auto\-update (Decided, 2026\-07\-23):** a Loan Account carries its own `autoUpdateFromPayments` toggle (§12), asked once at "Get loan" confirmation (§9.1) and freely changeable afterward from the Account's own edit screen — consistent with §3.6's additive\-not\-replacement principle: turning it on never removes the ability to correct the balance directly. **On:** the balance decrements each time the linked Recurring Expense sweeps. **Off:** the balance stays a plain, manually\-edited figure, same as Credit.

**The decrement recomputes from live state each cycle, never from a schedule fixed at origination (Decided, 2026\-09\-02):** `interest = Account.balance × interestRate ÷ 12`, and `principal = the swept amount − interest`. A one\-time amortization table computed at "Get loan" would be wrong from the first thing that changed — an extra payment, a corrected balance, a variable\-rate reset — and this loan is expected to change. **The decrement floors at zero, and reaching zero finishes the linked Recurring Expense immediately (§3.11)** rather than letting further sweeps drive the tracked debt negative, which §5.3 would then read as an asset.

**The amortization inputs are stored on the Account, not left inside a calculator (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — without them the feature is unimplementable as written).** §12's Account entity gains `principal`, `interestRate`, `termMonths`, `rateType` (`fixed | variable | interestOnly`) and `originationDate`, all Loan\-only and nullable. §9.1's calculator populates them at "Get loan"; they are editable afterward like any other field (§3.6). **This deliberately makes auto\-update available to a manually created Loan too** — a loan that predates the app is the dominant real case (§9.2 makes the same observation about credit cards), and scoping the toggle to calculator\-created loans only would put it out of reach of most real ones. A Loan with null inputs cannot have the toggle on; it is hidden rather than greyed, per §2.5.

**Editing a linked Loan payment's amount opens the Account's rate field in the same flow (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** §9.1 advertises a variable\-rate reset as a two\-tap correction via §3.11's edit\-and\-reconcile prompt — but that prompt edits the *Recurring Expense's* amount, and nothing propagated to `Account.interestRate`. Left unlinked, the balance would keep decrementing against a stale rate for the rest of the term and land nowhere near zero, breaking §5.3 for precisely the person it exists for. The rate field is offered alongside the payment change, pre\-filled with the current value and skippable. §3.11 additionally applies the confirmed reconciliation difference to `Account.balance` as principal, so the tracked debt doesn't drift by each reset's difference.

**Each auto\-update decrement logs its own Transaction** (`key: acct:<id>`, dated to the sweep's real occurrence date per §3.11's governing rule, payee defaulting to the linked expense's name). Without this, §5.3's ledger\-derived history shows the loan flat across every month it was actually being paid down.

**The Account ↔ Recurring Expense link is two\-directional (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** `Account.linkedRecurringExpenseId` is joined by `RecurringExpense.linkedAccountId` (§12), because the sweep engine iterates Recurring Expenses and previously had no way back to the Account it was supposed to decrement. **Both fields are available on Credit as well as Loan (extended 2026\-09\-02)**, since §9.2's payoff panel can now create a fixed\-payment Recurring Expense; a Credit link carries no auto\-update, only the pairing.

**Deleting either side of a linked pair cascades, with a prompt (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** §9.1 makes the Loan pair "obligatory and atomic once 'Get loan' is confirmed" but said nothing about deletion, which left a real hole: paying a loan off early and deleting the Account would leave its €410/month expense sweeping out of Savings until 2030, untouched and unprompted. The same hole existed for §9.2's Credit fixed payment, which has no `endDate` at all and would therefore sweep forever.

- Deleting **either** side shows an unskippable prompt naming the other — *"This also removes the €410/month payment"* when deleting the Account, *"This also removes the linked account"* when deleting the expense — with the usual Confirm/Cancel pair.
- If the Recurring Expense has an un\-elapsed swept amount, §3.11's reversal prompt applies as normal, in the same flow.
- Cancelling deletes nothing. There is no unlink\-only path: leaving one half alive is what produced the runaway sweep this rule prevents, and §9.1's "a decisive action is never allowed to leave no trace" cuts both ways — undoing it shouldn't leave half a trace either.

**Retyping an Account clears the state belonging to the type it leaves (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** Retyping a Loan to Credit forces `autoUpdateFromPayments` to false, clears the amortization inputs, and triggers the cascade prompt above for any linked Recurring Expense — a Credit account cannot keep a Loan's tracked figure honest, which is auto\-update's entire justification (§7.1). Retyping Credit to Loan clears `apr` and leaves the amortization inputs null, so the auto\-update toggle is absent until they are filled in.

### 7\.3 Full transaction ledger (Decided — architecture 2026\-07\-22, pair rules pinned 2026\-09\-02; screen built 2026\-09\-14)

**Transaction has no general CRUD of its own, with one narrow, scoped exception.** Every field a Transaction can touch — Savings, Goal.saved, Category.spent, Account.balance — is already directly editable via its own screen (§3.6, §7.2); a separate "edit the transaction itself" surface would duplicate that capability, not add one. So the ledger is, almost entirely, a pure, automatically generated, append\-only log — a byproduct of quick\-add, sweeps, auto\-deposits, catch\-up entries and direct edits (§3.6, §3.9, §3.11) — browsable, filterable (by target, date range, payee), and searchable, **displayed in `date` order**. Tapping a row deep\-links to the live item it touched for correction; there's no edit path through the log itself, and a single, standalone Transaction is never individually deletable.

**Pair\-deletion — the one, deliberately narrow exception (Decided, 2026\-07\-28; eligibility pinned 2026\-09\-02).** It fixes exactly one problem: a mistaken entry, immediately corrected by its own exact\-opposite entry, left permanently cluttering the ledger for something that never happened in the real world. A delete affordance appears on a Transaction's card **only** when all of the following hold — there is no general\-purpose single\-Transaction delete button anywhere:

- It has an **exact\-opposite counterpart** on the **same item** (same `key`, §12) — equal `amount`, opposite sign, per §3.6's sign table.
- The counterpart is the **immediately following** entry on that item in **`loggedAt` order** — no other Transaction on that same item was logged in between. **Ordering is by `loggedAt`, not `date` (specified 2026\-09\-02):** the same backdating loophole the 48\-hour rule was written to close applies just as much to adjacency, and a €40 entry backdated three weeks would otherwise sit between the pair in the ledger's display order while sitting outside it in real time. This means eligibility is computed in a different order from the one the ledger displays, which is intended.
- Pairing is **forward only, and each Transaction belongs to at most one pair** — a correction follows its mistake, never precedes it. Without this, a row with an eligible counterpart on each side would delete an arbitrary one of them, and a sequence of four same\-amount entries could be unwound in two different directions to two different ledgers.
- Both fall within a **48\-hour window of each other**, measured against `loggedAt`.
- **Eligibility is evaluated once, against the history as it stands before any deletion, and is not re\-derived afterward (specified 2026\-09\-02).** Otherwise deleting one pair can make two previously non\-adjacent rows adjacent, surfacing a new delete affordance that the first confirmation never authorised, and a chain of same\-amount entries could be unwound indefinitely behind a single DELETE prompt. §7.3's own framing of this as "a rarer, higher\-consequence action" argues for the bounded blast radius.

**What "reverses each Transaction's actual recorded effect" covers (specified 2026\-09\-02).** Deleting the pair reverses each row's real effect — not merely removing the log rows — so their combined effect nets to zero. Because the two entries already summed to zero the moment the second landed, **no current balance changes**. But a Transaction's recorded effect reaches past balances, and all of it must be undone:

- the target field (`Goal.saved`, `Category.spent`, `Account.balance`, `Savings.amount`) and the Savings movement, which net to zero by construction;
- **`lastIncomeLoggedDate` and `lastIncomeDepositDate`**, recomputed to the most recent surviving qualifying Transaction, or to `incomeEnabledDate` if none remains (§3.3) — otherwise the "no income logged" check stays suppressed for a full cadence off a timestamp that refers to nothing;
- any **Loan auto\-update decrement** logged against the pair (§7.2).

**`incomeReceivedThisMonth` needs no rule here (2026\-09\-02).** An earlier version of this list required it to be decremented when a deleted row was `isIncome`. That was written while the figure was a stored counter; §3.3 now derives it from the ledger, so removing the rows removes them from the sum with nothing to maintain. This is one of three special cases the derived definition retired.

**Pair\-deleted rows are excluded from every ledger\-derived figure** — §5.1's FIFO replay and §5.3's history alike. This does retroactively change trend points that have already been shown, which is the feature working as intended rather than a side effect: the rows describe something that never happened, so nothing derived from them should keep reflecting it.

Requires typing **DELETE** into an unskippable prompt before confirming — the same heavier\-than\-Confirm/Cancel register as Savings' full editor (§3.6), and for the same reason: this is a rarer, higher\-consequence action than an ordinary quick\-add, and it is the framing that argues for the bounded blast radius above.

**Recurring Expense sweep rows are never pair\-deletable (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — replaces a sentence that implied a path existed).** §3.11's anchor\-chain reversal remains the only mechanism for undoing a sweep, and its reversal Transaction is deliberately left in the ledger beside the sweep it reverses. The earlier wording said a swept row "can only ever become part of an eligible pair after it's already been reversed through that mechanism," but that path is essentially unreachable in practice — a reversal typically lands days after the sweep, failing the 48\-hour test — and making it reachable would mean widening a window this section calls deliberately narrow. Both rows staying visible is also the more honest record: the sweep really did move money, and the reversal really did move it back.

Since the log is never rewritten outside this one narrow case, each entry freezes a snapshot of the target's name at logging time (`targetLabel`), so a Category, Goal or Account renamed or deleted later doesn't orphan its own history.

**SMS/notification\-based auto\-detection of transactions — considered and set aside, not pursued (Decided, 2026\-07\-22; basis corrected 2026\-09\-02).** The alternative to manual/quick\-add logging is scanning bank SMS or notifications to auto\-detect transactions. The disqualifying obstacle is Google Play's Sensitive Permissions policy: an app requesting `READ_SMS` must be the user's actual default handler for SMS, Phone, or Assistant, not merely declare a use case — asking someone to make a budgeting app their default text\-messaging app is a wildly disproportionate ask, and apps that aren't a default handler can't even declare the permission. Windows 10/11 offers no equivalent capability to build the other half on. (This decision previously rested on an iOS comparison, which §1's platform scope makes irrelevant — the conclusion is unchanged, only its stated basis needed correcting.) Set aside rather than deleted from consideration, but not a live direction under the current shape of the app.

**The basis was wrong on both halves, corrected 2026\-09\-05 — and the conclusion no longer follows from it.** The paragraph above conflates two mechanisms that are governed differently, then disqualifies both with an argument that only reaches one of them.

- **Android.** `READ_SMS`'s default\-handler rule is correctly stated and it does close off SMS. It does **not** govern `NotificationListenerService`, which is a separate API behind a separate gate — a user\-granted "Notification access" toggle in system settings, with no default\-handler requirement of any kind. Reading bank *notifications* was never inside the rule this section used to rule it out.
- **Windows.** *"Windows 10/11 offers no equivalent capability to build the other half on"* is **factually incorrect**. `Windows.UI.Notifications.Management.UserNotificationListener` does exactly this: it reads all of the user's toast notifications behind a `RequestAccessAsync()` consent prompt that is revocable in system Settings, and `UserNotificationChangedTrigger` supplies a background task so it works with the app closed. It requires the User Notification Listener capability, and therefore a packaged (MSIX) build.

**What the real obstacles are, since they are not the ones stated.** Neither correction revives this as an obvious direction, but the reasons are different and worth recording accurately rather than leaving a true conclusion resting on a false premise:

- Android treats notification\-listener access as a **sensitive permission** and names it as frequently abused for financial fraud; Play Protect blocks sideloaded installs of apps declaring it, and the finance\-app policy area tightened materially through 2026. A budgeting app is not among the categories explicitly prohibited, so this is store\-review risk and a moving target rather than the hard gate `READ_SMS` presents.
- Neither platform can scope the permission to chosen senders. The **retention** can be scoped — non\-allowlisted senders discarded before anything is persisted, nothing leaving the device — but the **capability** granted is "read everything", which is a far broader ask than any other surface in this app makes.
- Windows requires MSIX packaging for the capability, which §1's JVM\-shipping desktop build does not currently produce.
- Parsing is reactive by nature: see §13.1's tested finding that each new class of misread is discovered by getting it wrong once.

**Neither correction changes §13.1's conclusion, and one strengthens it.** Notification listening was scored as a live candidate on 2026\-09\-05 and was then **dominated** — beaten on coverage *and* cost, at identical posture, by a Settings\-toggled reminder to import (§13.1). It leaves the front on the merits rather than on a policy technicality, which is a sounder place for it to sit. **What is retired is this paragraph's reasoning, not its outcome.**

## 8\. Profiles (~~Deferred — noted now, built later~~ built 2026\-09\-14, §8.4; status corrected 2026\-09\-25)

**Status note (2026\-09\-14):** the wrap this section specifies is built in the prototype — `PROFILE_WRAPPED`, the swap\-in/swap\-out on switch, create and delete — and §4.7's backup file is the array it produces. ~~The sub\-headings below keep their original "deferred" lead\-ins as the trail of when each was decided.~~ **Their lead\-ins now carry the built dates (corrected 2026\-09\-25).**

### 8\.1 What a Profile actually is (Decided; test corrected 2026\-07\-24; built 2026\-09\-14)

A Profile is for a genuinely separate pool of money — **someone else's, something held for someone else, or the person's own money bound by rules (legal, shared\-ownership, or otherwise) that make it wrong to treat as fungible with everyday Savings.** "Someone else's money" was the original test, but it doesn't actually cover every case already on the list — a sibling's shared inherited\-property arrangement is partly the person's *own* money too, just legally encumbered. The real test isn't *whose* money it is, it's whether something external (a trust, probate, shared ownership, a legal restriction) makes it genuinely wrong to pool with everyday spending — as opposed to money that's simply mentally set aside, which stays fully fungible and belongs in a no\-target Goal instead (§8.2, §6.3). Concrete cases: monitoring an elderly parent's pension and expenses to help them make sound decisions; teaching a child to handle their own money from an early age; a joint account with a spouse; a shared arrangement with a sibling managing an inherited property's expenses; an inheritance or trust fund not yet freely usable. Within any one Profile, Savings stays exactly one figure (§3.1) — Profiles are how the app supports more than one genuinely separate pool, not a second, more\-organized version of the same one.

### 8\.2 What a Profile is *not* (Decided)

A second "savings account" that's actually the same money, just watched separately, is **not** a Profile — it's a Goal with no completion condition (§6.3).

### 8\.3 Switching mechanism (Decided; location corrected 2026\-09\-02; built 2026\-09\-14)

**The Profile switcher's hook lives in Settings → Other (§4.7), not on Budget's top row (corrected 2026\-09\-02).** This section previously designated Budget's top\-row avatar as the hook point while §4.7 and §11.6 both placed it in Settings — three Decided sections giving two locations, which an implementer cannot build. Settings wins: two sections already put it there, and §8.4 defers Profiles entirely, so a switcher that will be used rarely and stays inert until §8 is built doesn't warrant top\-level real estate on the app's most\-visited screen.

**Switching runs §3.9's catch\-up on the Profile it opens (2026\-09\-25, from the audit):** a switch, the fallback after deleting the active Profile, and a restore each make a Profile active the way opening the app does, so one last open in June shows September's reset Categories, its missed sweeps and its own card. **Creating a Profile opens §11.1's screen 1 (Decided, 2026\-09\-14):** "Add profile" in Settings → Other does not take a name inline; it presents the same sheet the first open does — name, theme — and continues to the Currency landing and the new Profile's Budget home. Switching between existing Profiles shows no onboarding. **The two explanatory notes the prototype's inline form carried — what a Profile is for, and that money merely set aside mentally is not one — move onto the Profiles page above the "New profile" row (Decided, 2026\-09\-14), read before the row is tapped; the page's own "separate environment" note stays.**

Not a new privacy/security concept in its own right: this is local, single\-owner data, not an online multi\-tenant system with an adversarial other party to guard against, so switching doesn't need its own lock mechanism. If the device\-level lock placeholder (§4.7) is ever built, it applies the same way regardless of which Profile is open — that's a fact about how the two interact, not a requirement Profiles introduces.

### 8\.4 Scheduling (Decided, 2026\-07\-22 — full wrap, not a split; Transaction added 2026\-09\-02; shipped 2026\-09\-14)

~~Explicitly deferred to a later build phase.~~ **Shipped 2026\-09\-14:** the Room store keys every table by `profileId` (§12), `AppSession.createProfile` and §11.1's screen 1 make a Profile, and Settings → Other carries the switcher, rename, delete and "New profile" (`ui/screens/SettingsScreens.kt`). Originally: Explicitly deferred to a later build phase. The data model should keep Profiles in mind — state shaped so that wrapping **Savings, Goals, Categories, Recurring Expenses, Accounts, Transactions, and Settings in full** (appearance/accessibility included, not just the Income fields) under a `profiles: [...]` array later is a mechanical lift, not a redesign — without any structural change being made in this pass.

**Transactions belong on that list (added 2026\-09\-02 — they were omitted, and the omission would have broken the concept).** §5.1 and §5.3 derive two entire Reports from the ledger, so a shared Transaction store means the "genuinely separate pool" §8.1 defines isn't separate at all: opening the ledger in one Profile would list another's entries, the FIFO walk would consume lots from both pools, and Savings vs. Owed would net one person's loan against another's Savings. Entity ids must also be unique across Profiles, or `key: goal:<id>` collides between them.

A Profile is a genuinely separate environment, not just a separate set of financial figures with a shared device\-level shell — a parent's Profile can reasonably carry her own text scale or currency, exactly as it carries her own income figure, rather than inheriting whoever's device it happens to be viewed on. One wrap point, not a device\-level/profile\-level split. **ImportState (§4.3, §12) is inside the wrap (corrected 2026\-09\-14):** the prototype had left `importedKeys` and `payeeRules` outside it, which would have made a statement imported into one Profile arrive as "already imported" in another — exactly the shared\-ledger failure this section exists to prevent. The only state above the wrap is what a backup file (§4.7) carries beside the array: the schema version, the active Profile's id, and the two last\-export/last\-restore dates.

## 9\. Loan, Credit Cards & Credit Score (~~Deferred — sketched, not built~~ built: both calculators and the payoff outlook 2026\-09\-14, §13.3; the credit topic 2026\-09\-23, §9.3; the borrowing topic 2026\-10\-23, §4.5)

### 9\.1 Loan feature (Decided, resolved 2026\-07\-20; rate\-type handling and created\-entity fields specified 2026\-09\-02)

A decision\-support calculator, not just a balance\-with\-interest record — the explicit goal is to make the real cost of financing something vs. saving for it legible before the decision is made (e.g., financing a car now vs. saving for a used one later), reframing a purchase as a tool first, not a status symbol.

- **Inputs/scope:** principal, interest rate, term, and rate type. Fixed\-rate, variable\-rate, and interest\-only are all in scope — "anything that can mirror real life best," not fixed\-rate\-only.
- **Outputs:** monthly payment, total repaid, total interest, plus a side\-by\-side reframe — the same monthly amount saved instead, and what that would accumulate to over the same period.
- **Placement:** its own screen, also reachable from Learn (not an inline Learn\-card calculator).
- **Resolution paths:** "Get loan" creates a real Loan account plus a matching Recurring Expense for the payment, **obligatory and atomic once "Get loan" is actually confirmed** — the exploratory math stays fully available with zero commitment beforehand, but confirming is a decisive action gated the same way every other decisive action in the app is (§3.6's confirm/cancel pattern), and a decisive action is never allowed to leave no trace. "Save for it" creates a Goal; "Reset" clears the exploratory calculation, gated by the same unskippable confirm/cancel prompt (no undo button). Deleting either half of the created pair later cascades with a prompt (§7.2).
- **What the created Recurring Expense actually gets (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — previously unstated, and each choice compounds across the whole term):** `cadence: monthly`, `dateMode: fixedDate`, `dueDateAnchorDay` \= the origination day, and **`firstDueDate` \= `dueDate` \= one month after origination.** Real loans pay in arrears, so anchoring the first payment to the origination day itself would charge the person on the day they took the loan and shift the whole schedule by a month. **`firstDueDate` is the first *real* payment date, not the origination date** — it is the floor §3.11 uses to stop the occurrence count walking backward into the origination month and inventing a €410 payment that was never owed, so setting it to the origination day would defeat the field's entire purpose. **`endDate` \= the due date of the `termMonths`\-th occurrence.** `linkedAccountId` points at the new Loan; the Loan's `linkedRecurringExpenseId` points back (§7.2). Because the first due date falls in a later month, creation takes §3.11's third branch: nothing sweeps on creation, and `lastSweptMonth` is set to the origination month.
- **Both entities are named at the confirm step**, pre\-filled as *"Loan"* and *"Loan payment"* and editable. §12 requires a `name` on each, the calculator collects none otherwise, and `targetLabel` freezes whatever is chosen into every ledger row the pair ever writes.
- **All three rate types create a Recurring Expense (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — resolves an apparent collision with §3.11's scope boundary).** §3.11 restricts Recurring Expenses to obligations whose amount is knowable in advance and constant *between discrete, knowable changes* — a test all three pass, since a rate reset is a scheduled event rather than a per\-cycle surprise. The decision rests on this feature's actual purpose: reducing cognitive load. Refusing to create the payment for a variable\-rate loan would leave the person to build it by hand, which is strictly more work and more error\-prone than creating it and letting them adjust it at each reset in two taps. Each type differs only in what the app says and when it ends:
  - **Fixed\-rate:** `endDate` as above. On completion, *"Loan paid off — this Recurring Expense has ended"* (§3.11).
  - **Variable\-rate:** `endDate` as above; the swept amount is the payment as of creation. A plain note at creation states that the app can't know future rate resets, so the amount should be updated when one happens — §3.11's edit\-and\-reconcile prompt then handles the change, settles the current month's difference against both Savings and the tracked balance, and offers the Account's rate field in the same flow (§7.2). **Its completion copy is hedged rather than declarative** — something closer to *"This Recurring Expense has reached the end of its term and stopped. Check the balance with your lender."* — since a loan whose payments were reset part\-way is not reliably paid off at the original 48th occurrence, and §3.11's default line would be an assertion the app can't support. (Where auto\-update is on, the balance reaching zero finishes the expense earlier and the fixed\-rate line applies instead.)
  - **Interest\-only:** `endDate` \= the end of the interest\-only period, during which the payment genuinely is constant. **Its completion copy must not say "paid off"** — the entire principal is still outstanding at that point. Something closer to *"The interest\-only period has ended — this Recurring Expense has stopped. The principal is still outstanding."* Stated as fact, no warning framing. **What the term's end does to the tracked balance (Decided, 2026\-09\-25, with the owner):** only a loan whose payments amortise it is closed to zero by running to term — fixed\-rate gets a *"Loan finished"* `acct:` row taking its balance to 0, matching *"Loan paid off"*. Interest\-only and variable\-rate keep the balance they have, matching their own notices; the build had zeroed all three, so an interest\-only loan's notice said the principal was outstanding while §5.3 showed it gone.
- **The Recurring Expense doesn't sweep forever (Decided, 2026\-07\-23):** its `endDate` is populated automatically at the moment "Get loan" is confirmed, so the payment obligation ends when the loan is actually paid off rather than continuing to draw from Savings indefinitely. **An early payoff ends it sooner:** with auto\-update on, the tracked balance reaching zero finishes the expense immediately (§3.11, §7.2), rather than sweeping on to a term date fixed years earlier.
- **Confirming "Get loan" also asks whether the Loan Account should auto\-update (Decided, 2026\-07\-23):** a single yes/no alongside the confirm step, setting `autoUpdateFromPayments` (§7.2, §12). The calculator's inputs are stored on the Account (§7.2) so the principal/interest split has something to compute from. Changeable anytime afterward.
- **Default emphasis is always savings\-oriented, never credit\-oriented** — a deliberate, permanent stance, not a per\-user or per\-region setting: "Save for it" is the resting/default\-emphasis path on the screen. "Get loan" is always fully present and functional, never buried or friction\-gated beyond the standard confirm — choosing it is never treated as a mistake, no warning copy, no "are you sure, really?" This reflects the app's actual underlying point of view: debt, kept as close to zero as realistically possible, is the goal — but that point of view is expressed entirely through default emphasis and framing, never through blocking, grading, or valence color, consistent with §2.1–§2.3. Existing debt is a normal, manageable life situation, never an accusation.

### 9\.2 Credit Cards (Decided, resolved 2026\-07\-20 — two\-surface approach; creation path specified 2026\-09\-02)

Credit Cards need decision\-support in the same spirit as Loan (real\-cost legibility, savings\-oriented default, non\-judgmental toward existing debt) but the underlying mechanic is different enough to require a different shape, not a relabeled Loan calculator: a card is revolving (no fixed principal or term), and carrying no balance at all typically means **zero interest**. The real risk isn't "financing has a cost," it's that minimum payments can silently stretch payoff over years and multiply total interest paid, much less visibly than a loan's amortization table.

- **Surface 1 — payoff\-outlook panel, living inside the Credit Account's own screen (§7.1).** Ambient, ongoing, always\-there — closer in register to Buffer (§5.1) than to Loan's one\-time exploratory posture, since in the overwhelming majority of real cases the card and its balance already exist before anyone opens this panel. Pulls the Account's real balance; person enters/adjusts APR; recalculates live. Shows time\-to\-payoff and total interest side by side for at least: minimum\-payment\-only, a chosen fixed payment amount, and paid\-in\-full. Savings\-oriented default applies here too — paid\-in\-full is the visually primary framing, minimum\-only is present but never favored. **While the fixed\-payment field is empty, its projection and its Commit button are not shown (Decided 2026\-09\-26, with the owner)** — the build had floored an empty field to 100 minor units and shown *"€1 every month, never at this rate"*, a projection nobody asked for.
  - **APR is stored on the Account (`apr`, Credit\-only, nullable, §12 — added 2026\-09\-02).** An "ambient, ongoing, always\-there" surface that is blank on every visit because the rate wasn't persisted is not the thing this section decided to build, and §7.1's description of the panel as read\-only means it feeds no other math — not that it takes no input.
- **A chosen fixed payment may create a real Recurring Expense; the minimum payment never can (Decided, 2026\-09\-02).** This is the same purpose as §9.1's — letting the app carry an obligation the person has decided on, rather than leaving them to reconstruct it by hand — and it lands on the right side of §3.11's dividing line for a concrete reason rather than by exception:
  - **A fixed payment the person picks is a fixed amount on a fixed interval**, which is exactly what §3.11 holds. **It is created with `cadence: monthly`, `dateMode: fixedDate`, `dueDateAnchorDay` \= the commitment day, `firstDueDate` \= `dueDate` \= one month after commitment, and no `endDate`** — mirroring §9.1's created expense in every respect except the term, since a card's payoff date moves with every new purchase. As there, `firstDueDate` is the first *real* payment date rather than the commitment date, so §3.11's backward occurrence count can't invent a €200 payment in the month the person merely committed. Specifying these matters: the due date decides which of §3.11's creation branches fires, hence whether €200 leaves Savings today or a month from now. Confirmed with the usual Confirm/Cancel discipline. This is what turns Surface 1 from a projection into a plan the person can actually commit to.
  - **It is linked to the Account in both directions** (`Account.linkedRecurringExpenseId` ↔ `RecurringExpense.linkedAccountId`, §12 — both extended to Credit 2026\-09\-02), **and deleting either side cascades with a prompt exactly as the Loan pair does (§7.2).** This matters more here than for a Loan: with no `endDate`, an unlinked orphan left behind by deleting the Credit Account would sweep Savings every month forever. A Credit link carries no auto\-update — the balance moves with real purchases, not with the payment — only the pairing, which is also why §7.2's paired\-Savings\-outflow prompt defaults **off** on a linked Credit Account: the sweep already moved the Savings side.
  - **A minimum payment is a percentage of a moving balance, so it varies every cycle** — the precise case §3.11 names as belonging in Categories instead. It stays a projection on this panel and creates nothing. No exception to §3.11 is needed, because it was never inside its scope.
- **Surface 2 — a smaller, pre\-decision purchase calculator, reachable from Learn, same shape as the Loan calculator.** Answers "if I put this specific purchase on this card, what does financing it here actually cost" — genuinely pre\-decision the way Loan is, so it reuses Loan's exploratory posture rather than Surface 1's ambient one. **It creates nothing on its own**; where the person wants to act on it, it hands off to Surface 1 on the relevant Account.
- **The created expense is named at the confirm step**, pre\-filled as *"\[Account name\] payment"* and editable — §12 requires a `name`, the panel collects none otherwise, and `targetLabel` freezes whatever is chosen into every ledger row it ever writes.
- Both surfaces inherit Loan's non\-judgmental stance toward existing balances (§9.1) and the same confirm/cancel discipline (§3.6) for any action that would change a real balance or create a real Recurring Expense.

### 9\.3 Credit score education (Decided, resolved 2026\-07\-20 — hybrid, no computed number; demographic framing added 2026\-07\-22; classification criterion added 2026\-09\-02)

Credit score is real\-world consequential but not something the app can or should compute — it depends on proprietary scoring formulas, data the app doesn't have, and a legal/regulatory landscape that actively shifts by country and even by U.S. state. No calculator, no predicted number, anywhere. What the app can do is name the mechanism and the tension honestly, and point toward real authorities rather than trying to be one:

- **A Learn topic, meta\-transparent, not a calculator, not linked from either calculator.** States plainly that the app leans toward zero debt by design; that in credit\-scoring countries (primarily the U.S.), having no credit history at all — "credit invisible" — is a distinct, real phenomenon, different from having bad credit, and can itself create friction (e.g., manual underwriting, higher\-cost fallback credit); that this is a real tension the app's own stance creates; and that the app is naming it, not resolving it for the person. No "could be beneficial" framing, no behavioral recommendation — mechanism only.
- **Naming who this actually falls on, not just that it exists (Decided, 2026\-07\-22).** The phenomenon isn't evenly distributed, and describing it generically would itself misrepresent it. CFPB research documents that Black and Hispanic consumers are considerably more likely to be credit invisible or unscorable than White or Asian consumers, that people in low\-income neighborhoods are affected at roughly seven times the rate of people in upper\-income neighborhoods, and that young adults and recent immigrants establishing credit for the first time are disproportionately represented too — not because of anything they did, but because the on\-ramps into the scored\-credit system have never been equally available. Framed exactly the way existing debt already is in §9.1: a structural circumstance, never a personal accusation.
- **A passive, always\-present, non\-conditional note inside the Credit Card payoff\-outlook panel (§9.2, Surface 1)** — same register as §6.4's "Where this fits in your current frame": context, never a trigger, never phrased as a warning, no threshold to cross. Something close to: *"In countries that have credit scores, paying it off in full each cycle also adds to your payment history."* Always shown regardless of balance or payment behavior, so there's no version where its appearance itself reads as a judgment.
- **A collapsed, dated list of country\-specific resource links**, sitting with the Learn topic. Strict sourcing rule: **government/regulatory and central\-bank\-adjacent consumer\-protection sources only** — never a private credit bureau's own site (Experian, Schufa, etc.) and never a commercial/fintech blog, since those routinely function as lead\-generation for paid credit\-building products. Each entry is dated at creation, with a note that links are only as current as that date and that the person should treat this as a starting point, not a substitute for getting properly informed themselves.
- **The criterion that sorts a country between the two entry types (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — previously undefined, and the six entries encoded two incompatible readings).** The test is: **can an ordinary person obtain a score about themselves and act on it?** Not whether a score is statutorily standardized, and not whether the body producing it is public or private.
  - This is the criterion the second bucket's purpose demands. Its whole reason for existing is to avoid forcing countries into a US\-shaped "here's your bureau" template — so what matters is whether that template *describes the person's actual experience*, which is a question about what they can get and use, not about statute.
  - **The second bucket is accordingly labelled "no consumer\-facing credit score," not "no standardized score system" (relabelled 2026\-09\-02).** The old label named the very test this criterion rejects, and it was equally true of Canada and Germany — both of which sit in the *other* bucket and are themselves described as having no single standardized formula. Keeping it would have left the user\-facing copy asserting a distinction the classification no longer uses.
  - The previous entries got Canada and Germany the wrong way round relative to each other: both have competing private bureaus running proprietary models with no single national formula, yet one was filed as scored and the other as unscored. Under the criterion above, **Germany is scored** — the SCHUFA\-Score is exactly a consumer\-facing score a person can request — and its entry below is corrected accordingly. Only genuine register\-based systems, where no consumer\-facing score exists at all, stay in the second bucket.
- **Coverage is intentionally expandable\-over\-time, not complete\-at\-launch.** The mechanism (collapsed list, per\-country entry, dated, official\-sources\-only, the two entry types and the criterion above) is what's Decided now; the actual list covered on day one is scoped small — **the U.S., Canada, and four European examples** — specifically so that adding country \#31 later is adding a row, not a redesign.
- **First\-pass country coverage — researched and confirmed (2026\-08\-04; Germany reclassified 2026\-09\-02).** Six entries, each checked directly against its official source. Every link is a government or central\-bank page, never a bureau's own site.
  - **United States** — scored; source: Consumer Financial Protection Bureau, `consumerfinance.gov/consumer-tools/credit-reports-and-scores/`.
  - **Canada** — scored, with no single standardized formula: Equifax and TransUnion each run their own FICO/VantageScore\-derived model, so it isn't a clean US\-shaped entry either; source: Financial Consumer Agency of Canada, `canada.ca/en/financial-consumer-agency/services/credit-reports-score.html`.
  - **Germany** — **scored (reclassified 2026\-09\-02)**, with no single standardized formula: private bureaus, chiefly SCHUFA, each produce their own consumer\-facing score, which a person can request about themselves — structurally the same shape as Canada's, and previously mis\-filed on the strength of the word *standardized*. A nuance worth keeping in the entry itself: the financial regulator BaFin explicitly does not supervise credit bureaus or their scoring, so the correct government source is the federal data\-protection authority; source: BfDI, `bfdi.bund.de/DE/Buerger/Inhalte/Allgemein/Auskunfteien/FAQ-Auskunfteien.html`.
  - **Italy** — "no consumer\-facing credit score; creditworthiness assessed via the Banca d'Italia's Centrale dei Rischi instead" — ~~a public register~~ a Banca d'Italia register (kept by a public authority, but open only to the person and lenders; owner, 2026\-10\-27) of debts above a reporting threshold, not a score and not a blacklist; source: `bancaditalia.it/servizi-cittadino/servizi/accesso-cr/`.
  - **Spain** — "no consumer\-facing credit score; creditworthiness assessed via the Banco de España's Central de Información de Riesgos (CIRBE) instead" — same public\-register shape as Italy's; source: `bde.es/wbe/en/para-ciudadano/gestiones/informacion-riesgos-cir/`.
  - **France** — "no consumer\-facing credit score; creditworthiness assessed via the Banque de France's negative\-incident registries instead" — FICP for credit\-repayment incidents, FCC for cheque incidents; each bank then runs its own undisclosed internal scoring on top, which stays a fact worth naming rather than something the entry can point a resource at. Source: `banque-france.fr/fr/a-votre-service/particuliers/fichiers-incident-bancaire`.

**Built 2026\-09\-23, with the three things this section leaves to the build put to the person first.** The topic is titled **"Credit scores, and what this app doesn't do"** — it names the subject and the limit in one line, in the register of "Why nothing turns red", and says before it is opened that there is no calculator behind it. It sits **last** in §4.5's fixed order. The country list is a **second disclosure inside the topic's own body**, collapsed, which is what this section's wording asks for; the alternative weighed was a row opening its own screen — the pattern the two calculator rows on the same screen already use, and one that would have given six entries with their URLs more room than an accordion on a 360dp phone — and it was declined in favour of keeping the list where §9.3 puts it.

The body is five paragraphs, written from the bullets above and never from the prototype's card text (§14): the app's stance and the limit on what it will compute; credit invisibility as a state distinct from bad credit, with the friction it carries; the tension named and explicitly not resolved; who the CFPB's research finds it falls on, framed as §9.1 frames existing debt; and one line handing off to the list. **The source line stays as this section states it** — research by the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau on credit invisibility — rather than naming a particular report: the figures above are almost certainly the 2015 *Data Point: Credit Invisibles*, and "almost certainly" is not a citation, so the build did not print one it could not check.

**A standing limitation, recorded rather than solved (§13.1):** the resource list has no mechanism for staying current — nothing re\-checks a government agency's URL or name after the entry's creation date. This is a known, accepted limitation, not something the disclaimer text should be read as solving; it needs periodic manual review once built, with no automated mechanism planned.

## 10\. Overtime & Self\-Worth (~~Deferred — sketched, not built~~ built 2026\-10\-23)

A Learn topic (and an attached exploratory calculator) about the tendency to undervalue one's own time and labor — specifically, reluctance to ask for overtime pay, a raise, or other fair compensation, even when it's warranted.

### 10\.1 Scope: the psychological angle only (Decided, 2026\-07\-19; disproportionate\-impact framing added 2026\-07\-22)

Checked against real research before scoping this, consistent with §2.6's bar that every Learn topic trace to a named, citable source. Two distinct bodies of research came up, and only one is in scope here:

- **In scope — reluctance to ask.** *Women Don't Ask* (Babcock & Laschever) is the canonical citation; a 2025 peer\-reviewed paper in *Negotiation and Conflict Management Research* specifically models "negotiation avoidance" — people needing a disproportionately large perceived gain before they'll even raise the subject, and often paying a real cost just to avoid the conversation. Survey data on the *why* (fear of losing the offer/job, lacking negotiation skill, simply finding it unpleasant, low confidence) rounds this out. This is the angle the topic should be built around.
- **Explicitly out of scope — employer\-side wage practices.** Real, well\-documented (UK TUC data on unpaid overtime prevalence and cost; U.S. research on job\-title inflation used to dodge overtime law) — but this is a wage\-theft/legal\-entitlement question, not a self\-worth one, and drifts into giving what could read as legal advice, which isn't this app's place. Deliberately left out.

**Why this matters for tone, not just scope (Decided):** the explicit reason to scope this narrowly is that asking isn't always safe or possible — many people who don't ask for overtime pay or a raise are responding accurately to real risk (job security, power imbalance, exempt status with no formal overtime mechanism at all), not making an error to be corrected. The topic must validate that the reluctance is a normal, well\-documented response — not frame not\-asking as a mistake, and not create pressure toward a specific action. This is a direct application of §2.3 (autonomy\-supportive, never blocking) and §2.4's shame\-reduction register to a topic where getting the tone wrong could genuinely add stress rather than relieve it.

**Naming who this falls on hardest is accuracy, not exclusion (Decided, 2026\-07\-22):** An earlier pass of this section considered keeping the Learn topic's headline framing gender\-neutral, on the theory that naming women specifically might read as less inclusive of other readers. That reasoning didn't hold up: the wage gap, the pink tax, and a disproportionate share of unpaid household and financial labor are documented, not contested, and they mean the real risk being weighed — and the financial starting point being negotiated from — are objectively different for many women, not merely under\-discussed. Stating that plainly, in the topic's own hook rather than deferred to a citation footnote, doesn't exclude anyone else who recognizes the same reluctance in themselves — the broader negotiation\-avoidance research cited above already establishes the underlying mechanism generalizes across genders — it just refuses to flatten a real asymmetry for the comfort of whoever is least affected by it. The claim stays structural (documented, unequal circumstance) rather than a trait claim (a deficit in ability or confidence) — the same distinction that already keeps existing debt non\-judgmental in §9.1: naming who's hit hardest by circumstances outside their control is not an accusation aimed at anyone else, any more than describing who carries the most debt would be.

### 10\.2 The calculator (~~Deferred, sketched~~ built 2026\-10\-23; Income\-off path added 2026\-09\-02)

An exploratory, reflective tool — not a formal overtime\-pay claim generator, and never creates a real Transaction or Goal on its own (mirrors the Loan calculator's exploratory posture, §9.1, and §2.3's non\-blocking principle throughout). Shows what unpaid or unclaimed extra hours are actually worth, in the person's own numbers:

- **The rate is always directly enterable, and the calculator works with Income off (Decided, 2026\-09\-02 — closes a gap that made the app's default user unable to open its own Learn calculator).** The two branches below previously covered Salaried and Hourly exhaustively, and neither applies on a fresh install: Income is off by default, `income` is cleared, so `monthlyEquivalent()` has nothing to compute from and the Hourly type doesn't exist because §3.10 isn't built. §5.1 set the precedent when it deliberately generalized Age of Money to work without Income enabled "since a formula that only understood income deposits would quietly break that promise"; the same reasoning applies here. So the rate field is always present and always editable, and the two branches below are **pre\-fills, not gates**.
- **If Income is Hourly** (§3.10, itself deferred): the rate pre\-fills from Settings — extra hours × rate (or an overtime rate the person enters) → a monthly/yearly figure.
- **If Income is Salaried:** an effective hourly rate is derived from `monthlyEquivalent()` ÷ an expected\-hours estimate and offered as the pre\-fill — framed as *"here's what your time is worth, and what these extra hours add up to,"* never as a formal overtime\-pay entitlement claim, since many salaried roles are legally exempt from overtime altogether. **This is the only remaining consumer of an expected\-hours estimate anywhere in the app** (§3.10, revised 2026\-09\-02), so if that estimate is collected at all, it is collected for this.
- **If Income is off:** no pre\-fill; the person enters the rate themselves. Nothing else about the calculator changes.
- Reuses the same rate\-based math as Hourly Income rather than inventing a third calculation mechanism — and the Salaried path doesn't depend on Hourly Income being built, since it only needs `monthlyEquivalent()` and an expected\-hours estimate.
- Same placement pattern as Loan: its own screen, reachable from Learn (§4.5), not an inline Learn\-card calculator.
- **Confirmed unchanged (2026\-07\-22):** the math stays hours × rate regardless of who's entering it — the honesty §10.1's framing decision adds lives entirely in §10.3's surrounding narrative, not in the arithmetic. A pay\-equity benchmarking feature (comparing a person's rate against demographic norms) would need external wage data the app doesn't have and is a different, much larger feature — out of scope here.

### 10\.3 Learn topic (framing decided 2026\-07\-22; ~~copy not yet built~~ built 2026\-10\-23, narrowed against the sources)

Hook leads with the documented disparity directly, not a softened generality — something close to: *"Many people hesitate to ask for a raise, overtime pay, or other fair compensation they've earned — and for women, that hesitation is often a rational read of real, compounding circumstances: a documented wage gap, the pink tax, and a disproportionate share of unpaid household and financial labor all change what asking actually risks, and what it's negotiating up from."* Immediately follows with the generalizing move already established in §10.1 — the same reluctance, needing an outsized reason to even raise the subject at all, is documented broadly across genders too, so the topic stays squarely usable by any reader who recognizes the pattern in themselves without pretending the stakes are symmetric for everyone.

Cited research names Babcock & Laschever's *Women Don't Ask* directly rather than paraphrasing around it, alongside the 2025 negotiation\-avoidance paper (§10.1) for the broader mechanism. No employer\-wage\-practice content and no legal/jurisdictional detail (§10.1's scope exclusion holds unchanged) — the specificity added here is about who the psychological pattern hits hardest, not a wage\-law claim, so it doesn't reopen that exclusion. Would join the Learn topic list per §4.5 once built — hook, cited research, link into the calculator (§10.2) — following the same accordion structure as every other topic.

**Built 2026\-10\-23 — the prototype's text, checked claim by claim against the primary sources (research audit §2.5, added that day), and narrowed where they are narrower (owner: narrow or cut).** ¶1: the evidence for *rational* is backlash and lower returns (Bowles, Babcock & Lai 2007; Artz, Goodall & Oswald 2018; Pew 2023), so those are named; *that hesitation* went, because the later data find women ask about as often as men; **the pink tax was cut** (contested — NYC DCA 2015 against Moshary et al. 2023 — and not about what asking risks), **so was *financial* labour** (not established; unpaid care and household work is, ILO 2018), and so was *compounding*, which no source links to the risk of asking. This narrows §10.1's 2026\-07\-22 reading of all three as *documented, not contested*: the wage gap and unpaid care hold, the other two do not as stated. ¶2: the 2025 paper is Hunsaker, Zhang & Lee in *NCMR* 18(4), and it studies purchases, not pay, so the text says so; the reasons people give are Pew's, and its top one, being happy with the offer, is included. ¶3: *salaried\-exempt* became *no paid\-overtime arrangement to point to*, since a U.S. legal category is the jurisdictional detail §10.1 excludes. ¶4: *easier to think about clearly* went as unverified — the 2025 paper's hourly\-wage prompt did not change willingness to negotiate — leaving the number shown and the choice left alone. The topic links to the calculator from its last line, and Learn's Calculators list carries it too (owner, 2026\-10\-23). The calculator (`CalculatorScreens.kt`'s `TimeWorthCalcScreen`, `engine/TimeWorth.kt`) asks for the expected hours only with Income on (default 160), pre\-fills the rate from a month's pay over them as the field's placeholder, saves nothing and creates nothing.

* * *

## 11\. Onboarding

### 11\.1 Flow (Decided, redesigned 2026\-07\-24 — supersedes the original 4\-step implementation; screen 1's distribution decided 2026\-09\-14)

1. **Welcome \+ Theme, as a bottom sheet (Decided, 2026\-09\-14 — distribution and presentation chosen from seven scored mockups, `design/onboarding-options.html`, option E3).** The first page is a bottom sheet, so the screen carries no blank space: the sheet sizes to its content, and the ground behind it is the app itself — Budget home in the theme currently selected, under a scrim — so choosing a theme previews it live. **On the very first open that ground is the real, empty Budget home (Decided, 2026\-09\-14, later the same day):** the Savings row with its em dash, the wordmark, the tab bar — the screen exactly as it will look after Continue, not a drawn skeleton. **One column, in this order (name field and theme heading added later the same day):** the title *"Sci\-Fin stands for Scientific\-Finance"*; the description *"This app grows out of scientific research on how we perceive money. Its structure centres on saving."*; **"Name this profile"** and a text box; **"Choose a theme:"** with the caption *"You can always change it in Settings."*; ~~**the four themes in one row** — swatch chips and the one\-word name (Slate, Mauve, Wabi, Tide — §2.5's rename exists for this row);~~ **the four themes as a grid of two cards per row, no taller than 48dp, swatch and name (§2.5, 2026\-10\-07)**; Continue. No progress dots. **Continue waits for a name** — it stays disabled until at least one character is in the box, so no Profile is ever created with a made\-up name (the prototype's `'New profile'` fallback is retired); the box's placeholder reads *"Your name or a label"* (Decided, 2026\-09\-14, later the same day). ~~No heading over the row, no "change it in Settings" caption.~~ **The sheet has no handle and no dismiss gesture** — no drag\-down, no scrim tap, no Back — because this screen has no skip: Continue simply keeps whatever theme is already selected, which is ~~**Slate**~~ **Swiss Blue** (§2.5's stated default since 2026\-10\-07), so there is nothing to design a bypass for. **The sheet's sizes are proportional to the screen width** — on a smaller screen the cards and the text get smaller — so the row of four never wraps (measured: a card has 61px for its name at 360dp and 54px at 320dp; every one\-word name is 42px or less). **§2.5's text scale multiplies on top of that (Decided, 2026\-09\-14, later the same day — closes §13.1's item 2):** the sheet's sizes scale with the width, the person's chosen scale *(since 2026\-09\-25 the OS's font size — removed from the app 2026\-09\-25, schema 5)* multiplies the result, and the row of four wraps to two lines rather than shrinking below what they chose — `xl` always reads as `xl`. At 600dp and above the sheet becomes a centred dialog, as §4.8's rule 3 already says of every sheet. *What the engine measured, for the record:* the screen's elements were scored under §2.4's register (tone 0.35, no referent) across hero\+content, single\-column, card\-grid, two\-column and the sheet variants; the numbers separated only the prototype's own layout (0.65) from the rest (0.83–0.95), and could not see the sheet idea at all — which is why the presentation was decided from the mockups, not the table. ~~Originally: **Welcome \+ Theme** — one combined screen, split top/bottom. Top half: a single welcome line — one sentence naming what the app is for, in the register of §2.4, written here rather than taken from the prototype (§14); something close to *"A calmer way to keep track of your money."* Bottom half: theme picker.~~
2. **Notification permission** — the OS\-level system prompt, requested here (§11.5). **The page beneath it (Decided, 2026\-09\-14):** the prototype's — *"One more thing before the tour"* and *"Sci\-Fin can send a single, quiet notification on the 1st of each month — nothing else, ever, unless you choose to turn more on later."* — on both hosts. **Explain, then ask (Decided, 2026\-09\-15):** the page shows first with one **Continue** button on both hosts; on Android the tap requests the OS permission and its answer moves the flow on, on Windows, where §11.5 reads the system setting instead, the tap moves on directly.
